Taylor dispersion and phase mixing in the non-cutoff Boltzmann equation on the whole space
Jacob Bedrossian, Michele Coti Zelati, Michele Dolce

Abstract
. In this paper we describe the long-time behavior of the non-cutoff Boltzmann equation with soft potentials near a global Maxwellian background on the whole space in the weakly collisional limit (i.e. infinite Knudsen number 1 / ν → ∞ 1/\nu\to\infty ). Specifically, we prove that for initial data sufficiently small (independent of the Knudsen number), the solution displays several dynamics caused by the phase mixing/dispersive effects of the transport operator v ⋅ ∇ x v\cdot\nabla_{x} and its interplay with the singular collision operator. For x x -wavenumbers k k with | k | ≫ ν |k|\gg\nu , one sees an enhanced dissipation effect wherein the characteristic decay time-scale is accelerated to O ⁡ ( 1 / ν 1 1 + 2 ​ s ​ | k | 2 ​ s 1 + 2 ​ s ) O(1/\nu^{\frac{1}{1+2s}}\left|k\right|^{\frac{2s}{1+2s}}) , where s ∈ ( 0 , 1 ] s\in(0,1] is the singularity of the kernel ( s = 1 s=1 being the Landau collision operator, which is also included in our analysis); for | k | ≪ ν |k|\ll\nu , one sees Taylor dispersion , wherein the decay is accelerated to O ⁡ ( ν / | k | 2 ) O(\nu/\left|k\right|^{2}) . Additionally, we prove almost-uniform phase mixing estimates. For macroscopic quantities as the density ρ \rho , these bounds imply almost-uniform-in- ν \nu decay of ( t ​ ∇ x ) β ​ ρ (t\nabla_{x})^{\beta}\rho in L x ∞ L^{\infty}_{x} due to Landau damping and dispersive decay.



1.
Introduction

In this paper we consider the Boltzmann equation for a distribution function
F=F(t,x,v)
,

(1.1)

\displaystyle{\partial}_{t}F+v\cdot\nabla_{x}F=\nu\mathcal{Q}(F,F),\qquad t\geq 0,\,x,v\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\,(\text{or}\,\mathbb{T}^{d}),

where
1/\nu\geq 0
is the Knudsen number or inverse collision frequency. We are interested in understanding the long-time dynamics of this equation in the limit
\nu\to 0
, i.e. the weakly collisional limit.
That is, we describe the dynamics simultaneously in
t\to\infty
and
\nu\to 0
.
The Boltzmann equation serves as the classical kinetic model for the dynamics of a rarefied, monoatomic gas with
\mathcal{Q}
accounting for elastic, binary collisions between the gas molecules.
The collision operator
\mathcal{Q}
is

(1.2)

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}(f,g)(v)=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}B(v-v_{*},\sigma)(f^{\prime}g^{\prime}_{*}-fg_{*}){\rm d}v_{*}{\rm d}\sigma,

with

(1.3)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}v^{\prime}=\displaystyle\frac{v+v_{*}}{2}+\frac{|v-v_{*}|}{2}\sigma,\\
v^{\prime}_{*}=\displaystyle\frac{v+v_{*}}{2}-\frac{|v-v_{*}|}{2}\sigma,\end{cases}\qquad f^{\prime}=f(v^{\prime}),\quad g^{\prime}_{*}=g(v^{\prime}_{*}),\quad f=f(v),\quad g_{*}=g(v_{*}).

The velocities
v^{\prime},\,v^{\prime}_{*}
are the result of an elastic collision between particles with equal mass and velocities
v,v_{*}
. In particular, momentum and energy are conserved, namely

(1.4)

\begin{cases}v^{\prime}+v^{\prime}_{*}=v+v_{*},\\
|v^{\prime}|^{2}+|v^{\prime}_{*}|^{2}=|v|^{2}+|v_{*}|^{2}.\end{cases}

As in
[
5
]
, we consider the class of non-negative kernels
B=B(z,\sigma):\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}\to\mathbb{R}
depending only on
|z|
and the angle between the vectors
z
and
\sigma
. In particular, we take

(1.5)

B(z,\sigma)=\Phi(|z|)b(\cos(\theta)),\qquad\cos(\theta)=\frac{z}{|z|}\cdot\sigma,\quad 0\leq\theta\leq\frac{\pi}{2}.

where the kinetic factor is given by

(1.6)

\Phi(|z|)=|z|^{\gamma},

whereas the collision angle contains a singularity

(1.7)

b(\cos(\theta))\approx K\theta^{1-(d+2s)},\qquad\text{as }\theta\to 0^{+},\qquad 0<s<1.

More precisely, as in
[
38
]
, we assume no angular cutoff, namely for some
s\in(0,1)
we have

(1.8)

\sin(\theta)b(\cos(\theta))\approx\frac{1}{\theta^{d+2s}}.

See
[
20
]
for more discussion about the derivation of the collision operator.
The parameter
\gamma
differentiates
hard
from
soft
potentials in the following manner:

•

Soft potentials:
-d\leq\gamma+2s\leq 0
and
\gamma>\max\{-d,-d/2-2s\}
;

•

Moderately soft potentials:
-2s<\gamma<0
;

•

Hard potentials:
\gamma\geq 0
.

We will treat all of these cases, but mostly focus on the soft potentials, as these are the most difficult, since collisions have a weaker effect on high velocity particles.
The singular limit
s\to 1
reduces to the
Landau collision operator

[
18
,
67
]
.
In this limit, the collision operator becomes a second-order elliptic operator, which usually makes the analysis simpler.
This limit is important as it is a significantly more accurate model of charged particle collisions than Boltzmann, and hence is one of the standard collision models used in plasma physics.
All our results apply to the Landau collision operator (see Remark
1.3
), however, we focus our attention on the more difficult Boltzmann case.

The thermal equilibria are described by the global Maxwellians

\displaystyle\mu_{n,u,T}(v)=\frac{n}{(2\pi T)^{\frac{d}{2}}}{\rm e}^{-\frac{|v-u|^{2}}{2T}},

with parameters
n,T>0
,
u\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and all satisfy
\mathcal{Q}(\mu,\mu)=0
.
In this work we consider small (to be quantified), localized perturbations to a global thermal equilibrium, specifically we study solutions of the form

(1.9)

F=\mu+\sqrt{\mu}f,\qquad\mu(v)=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{\frac{d}{2}}}{\rm e}^{-\frac{|v|^{2}}{2}}.

The Maxwellian
\mu
is a stationary solution to (
1.1
) thanks to (
1.4
).
The equation for the perturbation
f
is given by

(1.10)

{\partial}_{t}f+v\cdot\nabla_{x}f+\nu\mathcal{L}f=\nu\Gamma(f,f),

where

(1.11)

\displaystyle\Gamma(g,h)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{{\sqrt{\mu}}}\mathcal{Q}\left({\sqrt{\mu}}g,{\sqrt{\mu}}h\right)

(1.12)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}f

\displaystyle=-\Gamma(\sqrt{\mu},f)-\Gamma(f,\sqrt{\mu}):=\mathcal{L}_{1}f+\mathcal{L}_{2}f.

In view of the conservation of the energy, we know
\mu\mu_{*}=\mu^{\prime}\mu^{\prime}_{*}
and therefore

(1.13)

\displaystyle\Gamma(g,h)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}B(v-v_{*},\sigma)\sqrt{\mu_{*}}\left(g^{\prime}_{*}h^{\prime}-g_{*}h\right){\rm d}v_{*}{\rm d}\sigma,

(1.14)

\displaystyle(\mathcal{L}f)(v)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2\sqrt{\mu}}\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}B(v-v_{*},\sigma)\mu\mu_{*}\left(\frac{f^{\prime}}{\sqrt{\mu^{\prime}}}+\frac{f^{\prime}_{*}}{\sqrt{\mu^{\prime}_{*}}}-\frac{f}{\sqrt{\mu}}-\frac{f_{*}}{\sqrt{\mu_{*}}}\right){\rm d}v_{*}{\rm d}\sigma.

From (
1.14
) and the conservation of mass, momentum and energy, it readily follows that

(1.15)

\operatorname{Ker}(\mathcal{L})=\mathrm{span}\left\{\sqrt{\mu},v\sqrt{\mu},|v|^{2}\sqrt{\mu}\right\}.

We will denote the projection onto the kernel as
\boldsymbol{P}
and for the perturbation
f
we denote

(1.16)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{P}f(t,x,v)=\left(\rho(t,x)+\mathsf{m}(t,x)\cdot v+\mathsf{e}(t,x)(\left|v\right|^{2}-d)\right)\sqrt{\mu};

the unknowns
(\rho,\mathsf{m},\mathsf{e})
correspond respectively to the macroscopic, or hydrodynamic, quantities of density, momentum and energy.

On
\mathbb{T}^{d}
, small data global existence near Maxwellians was proved for the Landau equations by Guo in
[
40
]
and for non-cutoff Boltzmann in independent works by Gressman and Strain
[
37
]
(extended to the whole space by Strain
[
61
]
) and Alexandre et. al. in
[
6
,
4
,
5
]
.
See
[
66
,
65
,
19
]
for some of the earlier works on Boltzmann with angular cutoffs and
[
42
,
43
,
34
]
for the corresponding stability and decay estimates.
Many further extensions of these foundational works exist, for example, covering cases which include self-generated electric and/or magnetic fields such as
[
41
,
33
,
44
,
60
,
32
,
31
]
.

The study of the
\nu\to 0
weakly collisional limit in kinetic theory is relatively new in mathematics.
However, in plasmas, collisions are typically very weak, and hence there are many works in the physics literature considering how the collisions and the phase mixing due to the free transport will interact albeit mostly in the context of Landau or Fokker-Planck collisions
[
52
,
62
,
55
,
56
]
.
If one considers the toy model

(1.17)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}g+v\cdot\nabla_{x}g=\nu\Delta_{v}g,

it is not hard to show that the
x
-Fourier modes
\hat{g}(t,k,v)=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{d/2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}{\rm e}^{-ik\cdot x}g(t,x,v){\rm d}x
undergo

enhanced dissipation
in the regime
\left|k\right|\gg\nu
, namely for some
\delta>0
there holds

(1.18)

\displaystyle\left\lVert\hat{g}(t,k)\right\rVert_{L_{v}^{2}}\lesssim{\rm e}^{-\delta\nu\left|k\right|^{2}t^{3}}\left\lVert\hat{g}(0,k)\right\rVert_{L_{v}^{2}}.

This was essentially observed by Kelvin
[
50
]
for the 2D incompressible Navier-Stokes equations linearized near the Couette flow and was predicted to hold also in plasmas due to charged particle collisions in
[
52
,
62
]
.
The effect here is that the free streaming
v\cdot\nabla_{x}
term creates large gradients in
v
of the size
\sim|k|t
, which correspondingly enhance the effect of the
\Delta_{v}
dissipation to
\sim|k|^{2}t^{2}
, which explains form in (
1.18
).
As the leading order singularity in the non-cutoff Boltzmann equation (
1.10
) is similar to a fractional Laplacian of order
s\in(0,1)
(see e.g. discussions in
[
4
,
37
,
58
]
), the rate of enhanced dissipation will depend on
s
in the corresponding manner; see Remark
1.4
below.

Another important effect observed in kinetic theory with
0\leq\nu\ll 1
is
phase mixing
and
Landau damping
, which generally refers to the rapid damping of hydrodynamic fields such as the density. This was first observed in the linearized Vlasov–Poisson equations by Landau in
[
51
]
and is now considered a fundamental aspect of collisionless plasma physics (see e.g.
[
57
,
18
]
).
For the kinetic transport equation (
1.17
) Landau damping
1
1

1

Often the term “Landau damping” is reserved specifically for the effect in plasma physics, however we will sometimes abuse terminology and use it here as well, as the origin of the damping is the same.
is relatively simple to verify, leading to the following estimates: for all
\beta\geq 0
,
m>d/2
and uniformly in
\nu
, there holds

\displaystyle\left\lVert\left|t\nabla_{x}\right|^{\beta}\rho\right\rVert_{L^{2}_{t}(0,\infty;\dot{H}^{1/2}_{x})}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\lVert\left\langle v\right\rangle^{m}\left\langle\nabla_{v}\right\rangle^{\beta}g(0)\right\rVert_{L^{2}_{x,v}}

\displaystyle\sup_{t\geq 0}\left\langle t\right\rangle^{d}\left\lVert\left|t\nabla_{x}\right|^{\beta}\rho\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}_{x}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sup_{k}\left\lVert\left\langle\nabla_{v}\right\rangle^{2m+\beta}\hat{g}(0,k)\right\rVert_{L^{1}_{v}}.

The first inequality follows from the Fourier transform formula for (
1.17
); see Lemma
2.6
below for a proof of the second inequality.
In particular, we see that regularity in
v
translates directly to decay of
\rho
.
Phase mixing refers to the fact that, provided there is some regularity of the initial distribution function, all of the particles are traveling at different velocities, which tends to correspondingly smooth out the density variations.
On
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, the decay is due to a combination of phase mixing and dispersion.

Landau damping for all sufficiently regular (analytic and sufficiently high Gevrey regularity) initial conditions for
x\in\mathbb{T}^{d}
was proved by Mouhot and Villani
[
53
]
; the Gevrey classes conjectured to be sharp was obtained in a later simplified proof
[
13
]
.
The analogue on
\mathbb{R}^{3}
, Landau damping and dispersive decay, was proved in Sobolev spaces for the screened Vlasov-Poisson equations in
[
14
]
; see also
[
45
,
47
,
48
,
46
]
for related works.
In
[
16
]
the enhanced dissipation effect was proved in the Vlasov–Poisson–Fokker–Planck equations on
\mathbb{T}^{d}
near a global Maxwellian, along with uniform-in-
\nu
Landau damping estimates on the density, provided the initial velocity-weighted
H^{\sigma}
norm of the initial condition was
\lesssim\nu^{1/3}
(see earlier work by Tristani
[
64
]
that proves uniform Landau damping for the linearized problem).
More recently, the case of Vlasov–Poisson–Landau equation on
\mathbb{T}^{d}
was solved by Chaturvedi, Luk, and Nguyen
[
21
]
.
Due to the nonlinear echo phenomenon, the
\mathcal{O}(\nu^{1/3})
threshold seems potentially sharp in Sobolev spaces
[
17
]
.
See also similar recent results on active suspension models for the collective motion of swimming of microorganisms
[
2
,
25
]
.
This kind of stability threshold problem mirrors the work on quantitative stability results based on enhanced dissipation in the incompressible Navier-Stokes equations (see e.g.
[
15
,
36
,
71
,
69
,
12
,
27
,
22
]
).
However, enhanced dissipation has probably been best studied mathematically in passive scalars (see e.g.
[
23
,
26
,
72
,
10
,
1
,
11
,
24
,
68
]
and the references therein).
For passive scalars, another effect arises, known as
Taylor dispersion
, wherein for
\left|k\right|\ll\nu
the effective dissipation rate for
\hat{f}(t,k)
of a passive scalar in a shear flow (in say, a channel) would be
\mathcal{O}(\nu^{-1}\left|k\right|^{2})
.
This effect was first predicted by Taylor in
[
63
]
; see also
[
8
]
.
The work
[
9
]
provided the first mathematically rigorous proof using center manifold theory, whereas
[
28
]
showed that a unified energy method could be used to treat both
\left|k\right|\gg\nu
and
\left|k\right|\ll\nu
. We will discuss this in more detail in Section
2.1
below.
This effect was also noted in the works on active suspensions in the linearized equations
[
2
]
.

The goal of this work is to prove that for sufficiently small initial data (independent of
\nu
), the Boltzmann equation (
1.10
) displays all of these effects: enhanced dissipation, Taylor dispersion, and a nearly-uniform-in-
\nu
phase mixing/Landau damping (discussed more below).
A careful reading of the work of
[
61
]
already shows that global existence of strong solutions holds for initial conditions small independent of
\nu
:
there exists a
\nu
-independent
\varepsilon_{0}>0
such that if the initial datum
f_{in}
satisfies

\displaystyle\sum_{\left|\alpha\right|+\left|\beta\right|\leq\sigma}\left\lVert\left\langle v\right\rangle^{m}\partial_{\alpha}^{\beta}f_{in}\right\rVert_{L^{2}_{x,v}}+\left\lVert\left\langle v\right\rangle^{m}\partial_{\alpha}^{\beta}f_{in}\right\rVert_{L^{2}_{v}L^{1}_{x}}\leq\varepsilon_{0},

for sufficiently large
\sigma,m
, then the solution is global in time and vanish as
t\to\infty
.
Hence, the specific aim of this paper is to obtain the quantitative estimates in the limit
\nu\to 0
.
Neither the methods of
[
21
]
nor
[
61
]
apply here although ideas from both are utilized; see Section
2
for an outline of the proof.
Note in particular that the Landau damping is not quite the same as (
1.17
) for
t\gg\nu^{-1}
.
More precise estimates that quantify the phase mixing are discussed in Section
2
.
Below we denote the density by

\displaystyle\rho(t,x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(t,x,v){\rm d}v.

Our main result reads as follows.

Theorem 1.1
.

Let
s\in(0,1)
and
\gamma
be either soft, moderately soft, or hard.
Let
\sigma>d
and
M,M^{\prime}>d
be integers.
There exists
\varepsilon_{0}>0
(independent of
\nu
) such that if the initial datum
f_{in}
satisfies

\displaystyle\sum_{\left|\alpha\right|+\left|\beta\right|\leq\sigma}\left\lVert\left\langle v\right\rangle^{M+M^{\prime}}\partial_{\alpha}^{\beta}f_{in}\right\rVert_{L^{2}_{x,v}}+\left\lVert\left\langle v\right\rangle^{M+M^{\prime}}\partial_{\alpha}^{\beta}f_{in}\right\rVert_{L^{2}_{v}L^{1}_{x}}=\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{0},

then the following holds for the corresponding solution
f
to (
1.10
) and for all
\nu\in(0,1)
:

(i)

For
\delta_{0},\delta_{1}>0
determined by the proof (depending only on
\sigma,M,M^{\prime}
), define

(1.19)

\lambda_{\nu,k}=\lambda(\nu,k)=\delta_{1}\begin{cases}\displaystyle\nu^{\frac{1}{1+2s}}|k|^{\frac{2s}{1+2s}},\qquad&\nu/|k|\leq\delta_{0},\\
\displaystyle\nu^{-1}|k|^{2},\qquad&\nu/|k|>\delta_{0}.\end{cases}

For an integer
J=J(\sigma,M,M^{\prime},\gamma,s)
there holds the Taylor dispersion/enhanced dissipation estimate

\displaystyle\sup_{t>0}\left\lVert\left\langle v\right\rangle^{M}\left\langle\lambda(\nu,\nabla_{x})t\right\rangle^{J}f(t)\right\rVert_{L^{2}_{x,v}}\lesssim\varepsilon.

A formula for
J
can be found in Theorem
2.4
; importantly, for any
N
we can choose
M,M^{\prime}
sufficiently large such that
J>N
.

(ii)

If
\frac{2s}{1+2s}J>d
, we have the low frequency decay estimate

\displaystyle\left\lVert\left\langle v\right\rangle^{M/2}\left\langle\lambda(\nu,\nabla_{x})t\right\rangle^{J/2}f(t)\right\rVert_{L^{2}_{v}L^{\infty}_{x}}\lesssim\left(\left\langle\nu t^{1+2s}\right\rangle^{-d/2s}+\nu^{d/2}\left\langle t\right\rangle^{-d/2}\right)\varepsilon.

(iii)

The density satisfies almost-uniform Landau damping, namely for
J
as in
(ii)
and all
\sigma-d>\beta>0
, there holds

\displaystyle\left\lVert\frac{\left\langle t\nabla_{x}\right\rangle^{\beta}}{\left\langle\nu t\right\rangle^{\beta}}\left\langle\lambda(\nu,\nabla_{x})t\right\rangle^{J}\rho(t)\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\lesssim\left(\left\langle t\right\rangle^{-d}+\nu^{d/2}\left\langle t\right\rangle^{-d/2}\right)\varepsilon.

Remark 1.2
.

Similar almost-uniform Landau damping estimates hold on other hydrodynamic moments of
f
. Almost-uniformity refers to the fact that one observes the same Landau damping as expected from the kinetic free transport until times
t\approx\nu^{-1}
, at which point the hydrodynamic fields are already
\mathcal{O}(\nu^{d})
.

Remark 1.3
.

All our results hold for the generalized Landau collision operator equation on
d=3
(setting
s=1
), i.e. the collision operator given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{L}(F,G)=\nabla_{v}\cdot\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\phi(v-v^{\prime})\left[F(v^{\prime})\nabla_{v}G-G(v)\nabla_{v^{\prime}}F(v^{\prime})\right]{\rm d}v^{\prime}\right),

where
\phi
is the non-negative matrix given by

\displaystyle\phi_{ij}(v)=\left(I-\frac{v\otimes v}{\left|v\right|^{2}}\right)\left|v\right|^{2+\gamma}.

The classical case is
\gamma=-3
(which is included in our analysis).
This collision operator is generally easier for our analysis than non-cutoff Boltzmann due to the local nature of the derivatives, which allows for a more direct use of hypocoercivity and simpler commutator estimates for the derivatives.

Remark 1.4
.

For
\left|k\right|\gg\nu
, the form of
\lambda(\nu,k)
can be guessed using the simple toy problem

\displaystyle\partial_{t}g+y\partial_{x}g=-\nu(-\Delta)^{s}g,

which can be solved explicitly using Fourier analysis on
(x,v)\in\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}
, yielding for some
\delta>0
,

\displaystyle\left\lVert\hat{g}(t,k)\right\rVert_{L_{y}^{2}}\lesssim{\rm e}^{-\delta\nu\left|k\right|^{2s}t^{1+2s}}\left\lVert\hat{g}(0,k)\right\rVert_{L_{y}^{2}}.

For
\left|k\right|\ll\nu
the rate is the same as the Taylor dispersion rate predicted for passive scalars by Taylor
[
63
]
.
It is interesting to note that this rate does not depend on
s
(or
\gamma
).

The proof on
\mathbb{T}^{d}
is significantly easier as one can adapt ideas from
[
21
]
to the Boltzmann collision operators (see especially Section
4
for what is required for this adaptation).
We state the result for completeness but will not discuss the proof further.
Note that
d=1
is possible in this theorem as the decay is not limited by low frequencies here.

Theorem 1.5

(Result on
\mathbb{T}^{d}
for
d\geq 1
)
.

If the initial datum
f_{in}
satisfies

(1.20)

\displaystyle\sum_{\left|\alpha\right|+\left|\beta\right|\leq\sigma}\left\lVert{\rm e}^{q\left|v\right|^{2}}\partial_{\alpha}^{\beta}f_{in}\right\rVert_{L^{2}_{x,v}}=\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{0},

then the following holds for the corresponding solution
f
to (
1.10
) and for all
\nu\in(0,1)
:

(i)

There holds the enhanced dissipation estimate

\displaystyle\left\lVert\left\langle v\right\rangle^{M}\left(f(t)-\int f(t,x,\cdot){\rm d}x\right)\right\rVert_{L^{2}_{x,v}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\varepsilon\min\left\{{\rm e}^{-\delta_{0}\left(\nu^{\frac{1}{1+2s}}t\right)^{p_{{}_{\gamma,s}}}},{\rm e}^{-\delta_{0}\left(\nu t\right)^{\frac{2}{2+|\gamma+2s|}}}\right\}

\displaystyle\left\lVert\left\langle v\right\rangle^{M}\int f(t,x,\cdot){\rm d}x\right\rVert_{L^{2}_{x,v}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\varepsilon{\rm e}^{-\delta_{0}(\nu t)^{\frac{2}{2+|\gamma+2s|}}},

where

p_{{}_{\gamma,s}}=\frac{2(1+s)}{2(1+s)+|\gamma|(3-s)-\vartheta_{s}}

with
\vartheta_{1}=0
and
\vartheta_{s}>0
arbitrarily small for any
s\in(0,1)
;

(ii)

For all
\beta<\sigma-d
, there holds the uniform Landau damping estimate

\displaystyle\left\lVert\left\langle t\nabla_{x}\right\rangle^{\beta}\rho(t)\right\rVert_{L^{2}}\lesssim\varepsilon\min\left\{{\rm e}^{-\delta_{0}\left(\nu^{\frac{1}{1+2s}}t\right)^{p_{{}_{\gamma,s}}}},{\rm e}^{-\delta_{0}\left(\nu t\right)^{\frac{2}{2+|\gamma+2s|}}}\right\}.

Remark 1.6
.

The stretched exponential decay rates can be easily explained by looking at the linearized problem. In particular, as shown by Strain and Guo
[
59
]
, this time-decay is a direct consequence of monotonicity estimates and properties of the collision operator. The bounds we state follows by a straightforward improvement (due to the Gaussian weight) of Lemma
4.8
and the proof of Theorem
2.4
. The rate
2/(2+|\gamma+2s|)
is the one that can be inferred for the Boltzmann equation
2
2

2

The stretched exponential rate obtained by Strain and Guo in
[
59
]
is
2/(2+|\gamma|)
for the Botlzmann equation and
2/3
for the Landau collision operator (corresponding to
2/(2+|\gamma+2s|)
when
\gamma=-3
and
s=1
). This improvement in the rate for the Boltzmann case is due to better bounds on
\mathcal{L}
obtained in subsequent works
[
37
,
4
]
, see Proposition
3.1
herein.
directly from the monotonicity estimate (
2.6
) and the properties of
\mathcal{L}
given in Proposition
3.1
. Notice that for
s=1
we have
p_{{}_{\gamma,1}}=2/(2+|\gamma|)
. Thus, for the Landau collision operator we get a power
p_{{}_{-3,1}}=2/5
which is an improvement over the
1/3
power given in
[
21
]
. We believe this improvement is related to the velocity weights we use in our energy for the hypocoercive scheme. In particular, we use a weight
\left\langle v\right\rangle^{-3/2}
for terms involving
v
-derivatives whereas in
[
21
]
a weight
\left\langle v\right\rangle^{-4}
is used.

2.
Outline of the proof

2.1.
Linearized problem

The first step in the proof of Theorem
1.1
is to understand the linearized problem

\displaystyle{\partial}_{t}f+v\cdot\nabla_{x}f+\nu\mathcal{L}f=0,

in the singular limit
\nu\to 0
.
Due to translation invariance, we Fourier transform in
x
, and obtain

(2.1)

\displaystyle{\partial}_{t}\hat{f}+ik\cdot v\hat{f}+\nu\mathcal{L}\hat{f}=0,

where

\displaystyle\hat{f}(t,k,v):=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{d/2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}{\rm e}^{-ik\cdot x}f(t,x,v){\rm d}x.

In analogy with the passive scalar problem
[
28
]
, the behavior of (
2.1
) is different depending on the relationship between
\left|k\right|
and
\nu
.
There are two distinct regimes:

\displaystyle\left|k\right|\gg\nu\quad\textup{the enhanced dissipation regime};

\displaystyle\left|k\right|\ll\nu\quad\textup{the Taylor dispersion regime}.

The most significant difference between (
2.1
) and the passive scalar problem (where
\mathcal{L}
is replaced by
\Delta_{v}
) lies in the Taylor dispersion regime, which will require
a more complicated energy method than the hypocoercivity employed in
[
28
]
. This is also reflected in the quantification of phase mixing, which we carry out through
the vector field method as done in several previous works (see e.g.
[
70
,
29
,
21
,
25
]
).
Define the vector field

\displaystyle Z=\nabla_{v}+t\nabla_{x},

and observe the commutation property

\displaystyle[Z,\partial_{t}+v\cdot\nabla_{x}]=0.

This vector field is exactly equivalent to the
\nabla_{v}
derivatives used in previous works that make the coordinate change
z=x-tv
(for example
[
53
,
13
]
), and so controlling
Z
implies the Landau damping of
\rho
and other hydrodynamic moments in exactly the same manner.
While it is convenient for treating Vlasov-Poisson in some contexts, the change of coordinates is not convenient for dealing with the collision operator, so it will be easier to work with
Z
instead.

\diamond

Enhanced dissipation regime:
\nu/|k|\leq\delta_{0}
.

In this range of
k,\nu
, we use the energy functional

\displaystyle E^{e.d.}_{M,B}(t,k):=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\alpha+|\beta|\leq B}

\displaystyle\frac{2^{-\mathtt{C}\beta}\left\langle k\right\rangle^{\alpha}}{\left\langle\nu t\right\rangle^{2\beta}}\bigg(\lVert\left\langle v\right\rangle^{M}Z^{\beta}\hat{f}\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}_{v}}+a_{\nu,k}\lVert\left\langle v\right\rangle^{M+q_{\gamma,s}}Z^{\beta}\nabla_{v}\hat{f}\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}_{v}}

(2.2)

\displaystyle\hskip-85.35826pt+2b_{\nu,k}{\rm Re}\left\langle\left\langle v\right\rangle^{M+q_{\gamma,s}}Z^{\beta}ik\hat{f},\left\langle v\right\rangle^{M+q_{\gamma,s}}Z^{\beta}\nabla_{v}\hat{f}\right\rangle_{L^{2}_{v}}\bigg).

where
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}
,
\beta
is a multi-index,
\mathtt{C}\geq 1
is sufficiently large depending on the proof,

(2.3)

\gamma/2s-\kappa_{0}<q_{\gamma,s}<\gamma/2s,\qquad 0<\kappa_{0}\ll 1,

where
\kappa_{0}
can be chosen arbitrarily small (
q_{\gamma,1}=\gamma/2
for
s=1
in the Landau case) and

(2.4)

a_{\nu,k}=a_{0}(\nu/|k|)^{\frac{2}{2s+1}},\qquad b_{\nu,k}=b_{0}(\nu/|k|)^{\frac{1}{2s+1}}|k|^{-1},

with
0<a_{0}\ll b_{0}\ll 1
fixed constants determined by the proof in Section
4
.
For the linearized problem (
2.1
) the
\left\langle\nu t\right\rangle^{-2\beta}
in the norm is only necessary in the Taylor dispersion regime (on
\mathbb{T}^{d}
it is never needed). Its presence is associated with problems emanating from low frequencies that then cause issues also in the nonlinear interactions.
Notice that

\displaystyle E^{e.d.}_{M,B}(t,k)\approx\sum_{\alpha+|\beta|\leq B}

\displaystyle\frac{2^{-\mathtt{C}\beta}\left\langle k\right\rangle^{\alpha}}{\left\langle\nu t\right\rangle^{2\beta}}\left(\lVert\left\langle v\right\rangle^{M}Z^{\beta}\hat{f}\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}_{v}}+a_{\nu,k}\lVert\left\langle v\right\rangle^{M+q_{\gamma,s}}Z^{\beta}\nabla_{v}\hat{f}\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}_{v}}\right),

where the implicit constants are independent of
k
or
\nu
.
There is no need to specifically treat the kernel of
\mathcal{L}
since ignoring
\mathbf{P}f
is equivalent to get error terms scaling as
\lVert\mu^{\delta}f\rVert_{L^{2}_{v}}
. In this regime, those are under control since a key point of the hypocoercivity scheme is to produce damping of
\nabla_{x}f
with the expected decay rate. This automatically implies the desired control over the hydrodynamic fields using that
\left|k\right|
is bounded below.
On
\mathbb{T}^{d}
, this energy is essentially all that is required to treat both the linear and nonlinear problem by Poincaré inequality; see
[
21
]
where essentially this method was used on the Vlasov-Poisson-Landau equations (i.e.
s=1
case with nonlinear electrostatic interactions) on
\mathbb{T}^{d}
.

The energy comes with a natural dissipation, namely, the negative-definite contributions that come from the time-derivative after the various coercivity properties have been used and the parameters
a_{0},b_{0}
have been suitably set.
This quantity is given by

(2.5)

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}^{e.d.}_{M,B}(t,k):=\sum_{\alpha+|\beta|\leq B}\frac{2^{-\mathtt{C}\beta}\left\langle k\right\rangle^{\alpha}}{\left\langle\nu t\right\rangle^{2\beta}}\bigg(\nu\mathcal{A}\left[\left\langle v\right\rangle^{M}Z^{\beta}\hat{f}\right]+\nu a_{\nu,k}\mathcal{A}\left[\left\langle v\right\rangle^{M+q_{\gamma,s}}\nabla_{v}Z^{\beta}\hat{f}\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 170.71652pt+b_{\nu,k}|k|^{2}\left\lVert\left\langle v\right\rangle^{M+q_{\gamma,s}}Z^{\beta}\hat{f}\right\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}_{v}}\bigg),

where
\mathcal{A}[g]
is an anisotropic norm naturally arising from the linearized operator
\mathcal{L}
; see (
3.4
) below for the definition.
It is comparable in some sense to certain weighted Sobolev norms of the type
\lVert\left\langle v\right\rangle^{\gamma/2}g\rVert_{H^{s}_{v}}
, see Proposition
3.1
in Section
3
for more details.
More precisely, for the linearized problem (
2.1
), we obtain the monotonicity estimate (see Section
4
),

(2.6)

\displaystyle\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}t}E^{e.d.}_{M,B}+\delta_{e}\mathcal{D}^{e.d.}_{M,B}\leq 0,

for a fixed universal constant
\delta_{e}>0
.
In the case of hard potentials, i.e.
\gamma\geq 0
, one can show that

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}^{e.d.}_{M,B}\gtrsim\lambda_{\nu,k}E^{e.d.}_{M,B},

and hence (
2.6
) implies exponential decay of the type
{\rm e}^{-\lambda_{\nu,k}t}
for the linearized problem.
In the case of soft potentials (
\gamma<0
), as is standard, one needs to a use a weak Poincaré-type approach, instead proving that for any
R>0

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}^{e.d.}_{M,B}\gtrsim R^{-q}\lambda_{\nu,k}E^{e.d.}_{M,B}-R^{-(q+2M^{\prime})}E^{e.d.}_{M+M^{\prime},B},

for some
q>0
depending on
\gamma,s
. Choosing
R
depending on
t
in an optimal way, one obtains then a suitable polynomial decay estimate

\displaystyle E^{e.d.}_{M,B}(t)\lesssim\left\langle\lambda_{\nu,k}t\right\rangle^{-J}E^{e.d.}_{M+M^{\prime},B}(0).

As usual, stretched-exponential decay can be obtained if one uses exponential or Gaussian weights; see Section
4.3
for more details.

\diamond

Taylor dispersion regime:
\nu/|k|>\delta_{0}
.

One of the major challenges in the low frequency regime, relative to the passive scalar case or the enhanced dissipation regime considered above,
is that we lose control on the kernel of the linearized operator
\boldsymbol{P}f
. Contrary to the passive scalar case
[
28
]
, where one can employ
a simple variation of the energy functional
E^{e.d.}_{M,B}
, we here have to combine hypocoercivity and a (quantitative) adaptation of the
micro-macro

energy approach introduced by Guo
[
43
]
, see also Duan et al.
[
34
,
35
]
and Strain
[
61
]
. An energy functional similar to
E^{e.d.}_{M,B}
is still used to obtain information on microscopic quantities, i.e.
(I-\boldsymbol{P})f
, however a different energy yields estimates on
(\rho,\mathsf{m},\mathsf{e})
.
Recall that
(\rho,\mathsf{m},\mathsf{e})
(defined in (
1.16
)) solve the following (non-closed) hydrodynamic system (see e.g.
[
43
,
35
]
)

(2.7a)

\displaystyle{\partial}_{t}\rho+\nabla_{x}\cdot\mathsf{m}=0,

(2.7b)

\displaystyle{\partial}_{t}\mathsf{m}+\nabla_{x}\rho=-2\nabla_{x}\mathsf{e}-\nabla_{x}\cdot\Theta[(I-\boldsymbol{P})f],

(2.7c)

\displaystyle{\partial}_{t}\mathsf{e}=-\frac{1}{3}\nabla_{x}\cdot\mathsf{m}-\frac{1}{6}\nabla_{x}\cdot\Lambda[(I-\boldsymbol{P})f],

where the high-order moment functions
\Theta[g]=(\Theta_{ij}[g])_{d\times d}
and
\Lambda[g]=(\Lambda_{1}[g],\dots,\Lambda_{d}[g])
are defined as

(2.8)

\displaystyle\Theta_{ij}[g]=\left\langle(v_{i}v_{j}-1)\sqrt{\mu},g\right\rangle_{L^{2}_{v}},

(2.9)

\displaystyle\Lambda_{i}[g]=\left\langle(|v|^{2}-(d+2))v_{i}\sqrt{\mu},g\right\rangle_{L^{2}_{v}}.

This system is used to design an appropriate energy that can transfer collisional damping from
(I-\boldsymbol{P})f
to
(\rho,\mathsf{m},\mathsf{e})
.
Here we would like to obtain the sharp decay rate predicted by Taylor dispersion and also obtain as much Landau damping as possible.
This motivates the introduction of the following energy functional

\displaystyle E^{T.d.}_{M,B}(t,k):=\frac{1}{2}\lVert\hat{f}\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}_{v}}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{|\beta|\leq B}\sum_{j=0}^{1}\frac{2^{-\mathtt{C}_{j}\beta}}{\left\langle\nu t\right\rangle^{2\beta}}\bigg(\frac{|k|}{\nu}\lVert Z^{\beta}\hat{f}\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}_{v}}+c_{1}\lVert Z^{\beta}\nabla_{v}^{j}(I-\boldsymbol{P})\hat{f}\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}_{v}}

(2.10)

\displaystyle\hskip 199.16928pt+c_{2}\lVert\left\langle v\right\rangle^{M+jq_{\gamma,s}}Z^{\beta}\nabla_{v}^{j}(I-\boldsymbol{P})\hat{f}\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}_{v}}\bigg)

(2.11)

\displaystyle+\frac{c_{0}}{\nu}\mathcal{M}+\frac{c_{3}}{\nu}\sum_{|\beta|\leq B}\frac{2^{-\mathtt{C}_{0}\beta}}{\left\langle\nu t\right\rangle^{2\beta}}{\rm Re}\left\langle\left\langle v\right\rangle^{M+q_{\gamma,s}}Z^{\beta}(ik(I-\boldsymbol{P})\hat{f}),\left\langle v\right\rangle^{M+q_{\gamma,s}}Z^{\beta}\nabla_{v}(I-\boldsymbol{P})\hat{f}\right\rangle_{L^{2}_{v}}\bigg),

where
q_{\gamma,s}
is defined in (
2.3
),
1\ll\mathtt{C}_{0}\ll\mathtt{C}_{1}
,
0<c_{i+1}\ll c_{i}\ll 1
with
i=0,\dots,3
are small universal constants determined by the proof; the term
\mathcal{M}
is a mixed inner product involving the macroscopic variables which we define precisely below.
The energy functional in (
2.10
) contains the terms in the enhanced dissipation regime for the projection out of the kernel (namely the ones multiplied by
c_{2}
and
c_{3}
). These terms do not give any information on
\boldsymbol{P}f
. To recover dissipation on
(\rho,\mathsf{m},\mathsf{e})
through (
2.7
) we use

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}:=\,

\displaystyle{\rm Re}(\Lambda[(I-\boldsymbol{P})\hat{f}]\cdot(ik\hat{\mathsf{e}})+b_{1}{\rm Re}\big(\big(\Theta[(I-\boldsymbol{P})\hat{f}]+(2\hat{\mathsf{e}}I)\big):(ik\hat{\mathsf{m}}+(ik\hat{\mathsf{m}})^{T})\big)

(2.12)

\displaystyle+b_{2}{\rm Re}\big(\hat{\mathsf{m}}\cdot(ik\hat{\rho})\big),

where
0<b_{2}\ll b_{1}\ll 1
are small universal constants determined by the proof. Notice that

(2.13)

\displaystyle E^{T.d.}_{M,B}\approx\,

\displaystyle\lVert\hat{f}\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}_{v}}+\sum_{|\beta|\leq B}\sum_{j=0}^{1}\frac{2^{-\mathtt{C}_{j}\beta}}{\left\langle\nu t\right\rangle^{2\beta}}\bigg(\frac{|k|}{\nu}\lVert Z^{\beta}\hat{f}\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}_{v}}+\lVert Z^{\beta}(\nabla_{v})^{j}(I-\boldsymbol{P})\hat{f}\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}_{v}}

(2.14)

\displaystyle\hskip 142.26378pt+\lVert\left\langle v\right\rangle^{M+jq_{\gamma,s}}Z^{\beta}(\nabla_{v})^{j}(I-\boldsymbol{P})\hat{f}\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}_{v}}\bigg).

Remark 2.1
.

The idea of adding the mixed inner product (
2.12
) in the energy to recover dissipation for
\boldsymbol{P}f

shares analogies with Kawashima-type energy arguments
[
49
]
; indeed one can use
\mathcal{M}
to explicitly build a
Kawashima compensator
.
For the Boltzmann equation, this energy method was introduced by Guo
[
43
]
.

As in the enhanced dissipation regime, for the linearized problem (
2.1
), we obtain a monotonicity estimate of the form

(2.15)

\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}t}E^{T.d.}_{M,B}+\delta_{d}\mathcal{D}^{T.d.}_{M,B}\leq 0,

for a fixed universal constant
\delta_{d}>0
and the dissipation functional

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}^{T.d.}_{M,B}:=\,

\displaystyle\nu\mathcal{A}[(I-\boldsymbol{P})\hat{f}]+\sum_{|\beta|\leq B}\frac{2^{-\mathtt{C}_{0}\beta}}{\left\langle\nu t\right\rangle^{2\beta}}\nu^{-1}|k|^{2}\bigg(\left\lVert Z^{\beta}\boldsymbol{P}\hat{f}\right\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}_{v}}+\left\lVert\left\langle v\right\rangle^{M+q_{\gamma,s}}Z^{\beta}(I-\boldsymbol{P})\hat{f}\right\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}_{v}}\bigg)

(2.16)

\displaystyle+\sum_{|\beta|\leq B}\sum_{j=0}^{1}\frac{2^{-\mathtt{C}_{j}\beta}}{\left\langle\nu t\right\rangle^{2\beta}}\bigg(|k|\mathcal{A}[Z^{\beta}(I-\boldsymbol{P})\hat{f}]+\nu\mathcal{A}[Z^{\beta}(\nabla_{v})^{j}(I-\boldsymbol{P})\hat{f}]

(2.17)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad+\nu\mathcal{A}[\left\langle v\right\rangle^{M+jq_{\gamma,s}}Z^{\beta}(\nabla_{v})^{j}(I-\boldsymbol{P})\hat{f}]\bigg).

Remark 2.2
.

Notice the
\nu^{-1}
prefactor in the last terms of (
2.10
).
This is possible due to the power of
k
and the uniform boundedness of
|k|/\nu
in this range of
k
,
\nu
.
This scaling is key to obtain the Taylor dispersion estimates in Theorem
1.1
.

Remark 2.3
.

The factor
\left\langle\nu t\right\rangle^{-2\beta}
is essential in our estimates in the regime
|k|\ll\nu
. As we show in Lemma
4.4
, it allows us to obtain dissipation for
Z^{\beta}\boldsymbol{P}\hat{f}
, whose control is necessary for the ‘Landau damping’. Having the factor
\left\langle\nu t\right\rangle^{-2\beta}
in the energy removes the effect of the phase mixing for
t\gg\nu^{-1}
, though this is a time-scale much larger than the one in which we see the dissipation enhancement. In Remark
4.6
, we comment about another natural strategy to get dissipation for
Z^{\beta}\boldsymbol{P}\hat{f}
, which however still requires the factor
\left\langle\nu t\right\rangle^{-2\beta}
. We do not know if this is sharp, but it might be an effect related to frequencies
|k|\ll\nu
where the phase mixing is very weak.

\diamond

Linear decay estimates.

In Section
4
we prove the following estimates on the linearized problem.

Theorem 2.4
.

Let
\hat{f}
solve (
2.1
) and
E^{e.d.}_{M,B}
,
E^{T.d.}_{M,N}
,
\lambda_{\nu,k}
, and
q_{\gamma,s}
be defined as in (
2.1
), (
2.10
), (
1.19
) and (
2.3
) respectively.
Then, the monotonicity estimates (
2.6
) and (
2.15
) both hold.
Define,

(2.18)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{M,B}(t,k):=\mathbbm{1}_{\nu/|k|\leq\delta_{0}}E^{e.d.}_{M,B}(t,k)+\mathbbm{1}_{\nu/|k|>\delta_{0}}E^{T.d.}_{M,B}(t,k),

(2.19)

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{M,B}(t,k):=\mathbbm{1}_{\nu/|k|\leq\delta_{0}}\mathcal{D}^{e.d.}_{M,B}(t,k)+\mathbbm{1}_{\nu/|k|>\delta_{0}}\mathcal{D}^{T.d.}_{M,B}(t,k).

Then, for any
M>2\max\{|q_{\gamma,s}|,|\gamma|+2s\}
and
M^{\prime}>1
there holds

(2.20)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{M,B}(t,k)\lesssim{\rm e}^{-\delta_{p}(\lambda_{\nu,k}t)^{1-p}}\mathcal{E}_{M,B}(0,k)+\frac{1}{\left\langle\lambda_{\nu,k}t\right\rangle^{\widetilde{M}}}\mathcal{E}_{M+M^{\prime},B}(0,k)

where
p\in(0,1)
is a given number,
0<\delta_{p}<1
and
\displaystyle\widetilde{M}=\frac{p(1+s)(|\gamma+2s|+2M^{\prime})}{|\gamma|(2-s)+2s|q_{\gamma,s}|}-p
.

Remark 2.5
.

By a straightforward variant, one can obtain stretched exponential decay estimates as in Theorem
1.5
using Gaussian localization estimates as in (
1.20
).
These are omitted as this is not possible for
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, which is the main focus of this work.

Before we continue to the nonlinear problem, let us briefly explain now how to pass from the distribution function estimates to the estimates on hydrodynamic quantities.
In particular, the decay estimate (
2.20
) implies all of the claims in Theorem
1.1
for the linearized problem (
2.1
).

Lemma 2.6
.

Let
\zeta\geq 0
,
\eta>d
, and
N
be integers.
For
j\leq\eta
, suppose that
b
satisfies
\left|\nabla_{v}^{j}b(v)\right|\lesssim_{j}\left\langle v\right\rangle^{N}
.
Let
d\geq 2
and let
f
satisfy
\sup_{t\geq 0}\mathcal{E}_{N+\eta,\eta}(t,k)<\infty
.
Then, there holds

\displaystyle\left\lVert\left\langle\lambda(\nu,\nabla_{x})t\right\rangle^{\zeta}\int f(t,\cdot,v)b(v){\rm d}v\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\lesssim_{\zeta,\eta}\left(\frac{1}{\left\langle t\right\rangle^{d}}+\frac{\nu^{d/2}}{\left\langle t\right\rangle^{d/2}}\right)\sup_{t\geq 0}\sup_{k\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\langle\lambda_{\nu,k}t\right\rangle^{\eta+\zeta}\sqrt{\mathcal{E}_{N+\eta,\eta}(t,k)}.

Proof.

The case
\zeta=0
can easily be extended to handle
\zeta>0
, and hence we only consider the following

\displaystyle\left\lVert\int f(t,\cdot,v)b(v){\rm d}v\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}_{x}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left|\int\hat{f}(t,k,v)b(v){\rm d}v\right|{\rm d}k

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{\left|k\right|\leq\delta_{0}^{-1}\nu}\left|\int\hat{f}(t,k,v)b(v){\rm d}v\right|{\rm d}k+\int_{\left|k\right|>\delta_{0}^{-1}\nu}\left|\int\hat{f}(t,k,v)b(v){\rm d}v\right|{\rm d}k

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(\int_{\left|k\right|\leq\delta_{0}^{-1}\nu}\frac{1}{\left\langle\nu^{-1}\left|k\right|^{2}t\right\rangle^{\eta}}{\rm d}k\right)\sup_{k\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\langle\lambda_{\nu,k}t\right\rangle^{\eta}\sqrt{\mathcal{E}_{M+\eta,0}(t,k)}

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{\left|k\right|>\delta_{0}^{-1}\nu}\frac{\left\langle\nu t\right\rangle^{\eta}}{\left|tk\right|^{\eta}\left\langle\lambda_{\nu,k}t\right\rangle^{\eta}}\left|\int\left\langle\lambda_{\nu,k}t\right\rangle^{\eta}\frac{Z^{\eta}}{\left\langle\nu t\right\rangle^{\eta}}\left(\hat{f}(t,k,v)b(v)\right){\rm d}v\right|{\rm d}k

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(\frac{\nu^{d/2}}{\left\langle t\right\rangle^{d/2}}+\frac{1}{\left\langle t\right\rangle^{d}}\right)\sup_{k\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\langle\lambda_{\nu,k}t\right\rangle^{\eta}\sqrt{\mathcal{E}_{M+\eta,\eta}(t,k)},

where in the last line we used that
\lambda_{\nu,k}\gtrsim\nu
for
\left|k\right|\gtrsim\nu
and we used
\eta>d
.
∎

In a similar vein, let us record the following
L^{2}_{v}L^{\infty}_{x}
decay estimate which follows from Theorem
2.4
.

Lemma 2.7
.

Let
\zeta\geq 0
,
\eta>d
, and
N
be integers.
For
j\leq\eta
, suppose that
b
satisfies
\left|\nabla_{v}^{j}b(v)\right|\lesssim_{j}\left\langle v\right\rangle^{N}
.
Let
d\geq 2
and let
f
satisfy
\sup_{t\geq 0}\mathcal{E}_{N+d,\zeta}(t,k)<\infty
.
Suppose further that
\frac{2s}{1+2s}\eta>d
. Then,

\displaystyle\left\lVert\left\langle\lambda(\nu,\nabla_{x})\right\rangle^{\zeta}bf\right\rVert_{L^{2}_{v}L^{\infty}_{x}}\lesssim\left(\frac{\nu^{d/2}}{\left\langle t\right\rangle^{d/2}}+\frac{1}{\left\langle\nu t^{1+2s}\right\rangle^{d/2s}}\right)\sup_{k\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\langle\lambda_{\nu,k}t\right\rangle^{\eta+\zeta}\sqrt{\mathcal{E}_{N+\eta}(t,k)}.

Proof.

The
\zeta>0
case is a trivial extension of the
\zeta=0
case, and so we just consider this one.
For
t\lesssim\nu^{-\frac{1}{1+2s}}
, we may simply use Sobolev embedding in
x
and obtain an estimate with no time-decay.
Therefore, consider
t\gtrsim\nu^{-\frac{1}{1+2s}}
.
By Cauchy-Schwarz and Fubini’s theorem,

\displaystyle\left\lVert bf\right\rVert_{L^{2}_{v}L^{\infty}_{x}}^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}b(v)\hat{f}(t,k,v){\rm d}k\right|^{2}{\rm d}v

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\langle v\right\rangle^{2N+\eta}\left|\hat{f}(t,k,v)\right|^{2}{\rm d}v{\rm d}k

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(\int_{\left|k\right|<\nu/\delta_{0}}\frac{1}{\left\langle\nu^{-1}|k|^{2}t\right\rangle^{\eta}}{\rm d}k+\int_{\left|k\right|\geq\nu/\delta_{0}}\frac{1}{\left\langle\nu^{\frac{1}{1+2s}}|k|^{\frac{2s}{1+2s}}t\right\rangle^{\eta}}{\rm d}k\right)\sup_{k\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\langle\lambda_{\nu,k}t\right\rangle^{\eta}\sqrt{\mathcal{E}_{N+\eta}(t,k)},

from which the desired estimate follows.
∎

2.2.
Nonlinear problem

For the nonlinear problem, we need also to estimate
\nu\Gamma(f,f)
, which now couples all of the
x
-frequencies together.
The norms we will use to treat the nonlinear problem are the following: for integers
M,M^{\prime},M_{J^{\prime}}>2d
,
B>B^{\prime}+d>3d
we define

(2.21a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(t)

\displaystyle:=\int_{\left|k\right|\leq\delta_{0}^{-1}\nu}\left\langle\lambda_{\nu,k}t\right\rangle^{2J}E^{T.d.}_{M,B}(t,k){\rm d}k+\int_{\left|k\right|>\delta_{0}^{-1}\nu}\left\langle\lambda_{\nu,k}t\right\rangle^{2J}E^{e.d.}_{M,B}(t,k){\rm d}k

(2.21b)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{mom}(t)

\displaystyle:=\int_{\left|k\right|\leq\delta_{0}^{-1}\nu}E^{T.d.}_{M+M^{\prime},B}(t,k){\rm d}k+\int_{\left|k\right|>\delta_{0}^{-1}\nu}E^{e.d.}_{M+M^{\prime},B}(t,k){\rm d}k

(2.21c)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{LF}(t)

\displaystyle:=\sup_{k:\left|k\right|\leq\delta_{0}^{-1}\nu}\left\langle\lambda_{\nu,k}t\right\rangle^{2J^{\prime}}E^{T.d.}_{M^{\prime},B^{\prime}}(t,k)+\sup_{k:\left|k\right|>\delta_{0}^{-1}\nu}\left\langle\lambda_{\nu,k}t\right\rangle^{2J^{\prime}}E^{e.d.}_{M^{\prime},B^{\prime}}(t,k)

(2.21d)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{LF,mom}(t)

\displaystyle:=\sup_{k:\left|k\right|\leq\delta_{0}^{-1}\nu}E^{T.d.}_{M^{\prime}+M_{J^{\prime}},B^{\prime}}(t,k)+\sup_{k:\left|k\right|>\delta_{0}^{-1}\nu}E^{e.d.}_{M^{\prime}+M_{J^{\prime}},B^{\prime}}(t,k),

where the parameters are set satisfying certain conditions determined by the proof, namely

\displaystyle M,M^{\prime}>2\max\{(|\gamma|(2-s)+2s|q_{\gamma,s}|)/(1+s),B+|\gamma|+2s\},

(2.22)

\displaystyle M_{J},M_{J^{\prime}}>2\max\{(|\gamma|(2-s)+2s|q_{\gamma,s}|)/(1+s),B+|\gamma|+2s\}(J+1),

(2.23)

\displaystyle M^{\prime}+M_{J^{\prime}}\leq M,\quad 2d<B^{\prime}<B-1,\quad d<J^{\prime}<J-1.

As above, each of these energies are also associated with a natural dissipation functional
\mathcal{D}_{\ast}
; see (
5.5
) and Section
4
.
The energy
\mathcal{E}
represents the fundamental
L^{2}_{x}L^{2}_{v}
decay estimates we obtain, while the energy
\mathcal{E}_{mom}
controls higher moments in
v
but with no decay estimates (to use a weak Poincaré approach in the treatment of soft potentials) and
\mathcal{E}_{LF}
obtains better estimates at low frequencies using
L^{\infty}_{k}L^{2}_{v}
(i.e. pointwise-in-frequency) as a more convenient surrogate for
L^{1}_{x}L^{2}_{v}
-type estimates. This allows to obtain the sharp decay estimates for both
f
and
\rho
; these sharp decay estimates are also crucial to close the nonlinear argument in
d=2
(however in
d\geq 3
, they can be obtained a posteriori).

The majority of the paper is devoted to the following bootstrap estimate, which implies Theorem
1.1
by a straightforward regularization argument.

Proposition 2.8
.

Let
f
be a classical solution to (
1.10
) such that
\left\langle v\right\rangle^{N}\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\partial_{v}^{\beta}f\in L^{2}_{x,v}
for all
t\in[0,T]
and
N,\alpha,\beta\geq 0
.
Suppose that for
t\in[0,T]
there holds

(2.24)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(t)+\nu\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{D}(\tau){\rm d}\tau

\displaystyle\leq 4\mathsf{B}_{0}\varepsilon^{2}

(2.25)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{mom}(t)+\nu\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{D}_{mom}(\tau){\rm d}\tau

\displaystyle\leq 4\mathsf{B}_{1}\varepsilon^{2}

(2.26)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{LF}(t)+\nu\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{D}_{LF}(\tau){\rm d}\tau

\displaystyle\leq 4\mathsf{B}_{2}\varepsilon^{2}

(2.27)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{mom,LF}(t)+\nu\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{D}_{mom,LF}(\tau){\rm d}\tau

\displaystyle\leq 4\mathsf{B}_{3}\varepsilon^{2},

for universal constants
\mathsf{B}_{j}
set by the proof.
Then for
\varepsilon
sufficiently small (not depending on
t
or
\nu
), the same estimates hold with
4
replaced with
2
.
Furthermore, the quantities on the left-hand side take values continuously in time, and therefore, these estimates hold for all
t\in[0,\infty)
.

Moving from the linearized to the nonlinear problem is not too difficult on
\mathbb{T}^{d}
, as the nonlinearity
\nu\Gamma(f,f)
has a power of
\nu
in front admits estimates of the general form

(2.28)

\displaystyle\left|\left\langle Z^{\beta}\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\Gamma,Z^{\beta}\partial_{x}^{\alpha}f\right\rangle\right|\lesssim\sqrt{\mathcal{E}}\mathcal{D},

where
\mathcal{E}
is one of the energy functionals and
\mathcal{D}
denotes one the associated dissipation operators.
This allows it to be absorbed by the dissipation in a relatively straightforward manner.
However, on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, several new difficulties arise connected with the low frequencies if one wants to obtain the sharp decay rates and almost-uniform Landau damping.
Instead of (
2.28
), we eventually reduce ourselves to an estimate of the form

\displaystyle\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}t}\mathcal{E}+\delta_{\star}\mathcal{D}\lesssim\sqrt{\mathcal{E}}\mathcal{D}+\left(\nu^{d}\mathbf{1}_{t\leq\nu^{-1}}+\frac{\nu^{d/2}}{\left\langle t\right\rangle^{d/2}}\mathbf{1}_{t>\nu^{-1}}\right)\sqrt{\mathcal{E}_{LF}}\sqrt{\mathcal{E}}\sqrt{\mathcal{D}}.

Coupled with suitable estimates on
\mathcal{E}_{LF}
and
\mathcal{E}_{mom}
provided
d\geq 2
, this implies Proposition
2.8
and hence Theorem
1.1
.

2.3.
Notation

Let
\beta=[\beta_{1},\dots,\beta_{d}]
be a multi-index with
\beta_{i}\in\mathbb{N}
and
|\beta|=\beta_{1}+\dots+\beta_{d}
. We define

(2.29)

\displaystyle Z^{\beta}=({\partial}_{v_{1}}+t{\partial}_{x_{1}})^{\beta_{1}}\dots({\partial}_{v_{d}}+t{\partial}_{x_{d}})^{\beta_{d}},

(2.30)

\displaystyle(\nabla_{v})^{\beta}={\partial}_{v_{1}}^{\beta_{1}}\dots{\partial}_{v_{d}}^{\beta_{d}},

(2.31)

\displaystyle(\nabla_{x})^{\beta}={\partial}_{x_{1}}^{\beta_{1}}\dots{\partial}_{x_{d}}^{\beta_{d}}.

We denote
\left\langle\nabla_{x}\right\rangle
as the operator whose symbol in the Fourier space is
\left\langle k\right\rangle=(1+|k|^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}}
. For a given constant
c>0
and a multi-index
\beta
, we will slightly abuse in notation by writing
c^{\beta}
instead of
c^{|\beta|}
.

The
L^{2}_{v}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
inner product inner product and norm are

(2.32)

\left\langle g,h\right\rangle_{L^{2}_{v}}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}g(v)\bar{h}(v){\rm d}v,\qquad\left\lVert g\right\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}_{v}}=\left\langle g,g\right\rangle_{L^{2}_{v}}.

We use the following notation for weighted Sobolev spaces

(2.33)

\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{H^{s}_{v,q}}=\left\lVert\left\langle v\right\rangle^{q}g\right\rVert_{H^{s}_{v}},

where
H^{s}_{v}
is the usual
H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
Sobolev space. When no confunsion arise, we will omit the subscript
v
for the weighted spaces.

We denote the
x
-Fourier transform as

(2.34)

\mathcal{F}(g)(k)=\hat{g}(k)=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{d/2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}{\rm e}^{-ix\cdot k}g(x){\rm d}x.

Given
\chi\in C^{\infty}_{c}(B_{2}(0))
with
\chi(k)=1
for
|k|\leq 1
we define the homogeneous Littlewood-Paley decomposition in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
as

(2.35)

g=\sum_{N\in 2^{\mathbb{Z}}}g_{N}

where
2^{\mathbb{Z}}=\{2^{j}:\,j\in\mathbb{Z}\}
is the set of dyadic numbers and

(2.36)

\widehat{g_{N}}(k)=(\chi(k/N)-\chi(2k/N))\hat{g}(k).

We denote

(2.37)

\displaystyle\widehat{P_{\leq N}g}(k)=\widehat{g_{\leq N}}(k)=\chi(k/N)\hat{g}(k),

(2.38)

\displaystyle\widehat{P_{>N}g}(k)=\widehat{g_{>N}}(k)=(1-\chi(k/N))\hat{g}(k).

Moreover

(2.39)

\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{L^{2}_{x}}=\left\lVert\hat{g}\right\rVert_{L^{2}_{k}}\approx\sum_{N\in 2^{\mathbb{Z}}}\left\lVert\widehat{g_{N}}\right\rVert_{L^{2}_{k}}.

3.
Preliminaries

In this section, we recall some known results and we prove basic estimates that play a crucial role in our subsequent analysis.

3.1.
Linearized operator

A well-known coercive estimate for the linearized operator, given in
[
54
]
, is

(3.1)

\left\langle\mathcal{L}g,g\right\rangle_{L^{2}_{v}}\geq C\left\lVert(I-\boldsymbol{P})g\right\rVert_{L^{2}_{v,\gamma/2}}^{2}.

This estimate shows a dissipative mechanism of the linearized operator for elements outside the kernel. However, it does not quantify any regularization property, which are in fact carried over by
\mathcal{L}_{1}=-\Gamma(\sqrt{\mu},\cdot)
.
In particular, by standard symmetrization arguments one can show

(3.2)

\displaystyle\left\langle\mathcal{L}_{1}g,g\right\rangle_{L^{2}_{v}}=\frac{1}{2}\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}B\left(\sqrt{\mu_{*}\mu^{\prime}_{*}}\left(g^{\prime}-g\right)^{2}+g^{2}_{*}\left(\sqrt{\mu}-\sqrt{\mu^{\prime}}\right)^{2}\right){\rm d}v_{*}{\rm d}v{\rm d}\sigma.

This identity suggests the following splitting, used for instance in
[
4
,
5
]
,

(3.3)

\left\langle\mathcal{L}g,g\right\rangle_{L^{2}_{v}}=\mathcal{A}[g]+\mathcal{K}[g],

where

(3.4)

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}[g]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}B\left(\mu_{*}\left(g^{\prime}-g\right)^{2}+g^{2}_{*}\left(\sqrt{\mu^{\prime}}-\sqrt{\mu}\right)^{2}\right){\rm d}v_{*}{\rm d}v{\rm d}\sigma

(3.5)

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}[g]

\displaystyle=\left\langle\mathcal{L}_{2}g,g\right\rangle_{L^{2}_{v}}+\frac{1}{2}\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}B\sqrt{\mu_{*}}\left(\sqrt{\mu^{\prime}_{*}}-\sqrt{\mu_{*}}\right)\left(g^{\prime}-g\right)^{2}{\rm d}v_{*}{\rm d}v{\rm d}\sigma.

From the results in
[
5
]
, which we recall below, we know that the operator
\mathcal{A}
contains the information about the anisotropic dissipation and is comparable to some weighted Sobolev norms. On the other hand,
\mathcal{K}
can be thought as a compact perturbation.
In the sequel, we also need to use the following variation of
\mathcal{A}

(3.6)

\mathcal{A}^{\kappa}[f]=\frac{1}{2}\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}B\left(\mu_{*}^{\kappa}\left(f^{\prime}-f\right)^{2}+f^{2}_{*}\left(\left(\mu^{\prime}\right)^{\kappa/2}-\mu^{\kappa/2}\right)^{2}\right){\rm d}v_{*}{\rm d}v{\rm d}\sigma

which is defined for any
\kappa>0
.

In the next proposition we collect some bounds that are proved in
[
5
, Proposition 2.1-2.2, Lemma 2.12 and Lemma 2.15]

(in the case of the Landau collision operator, i.e.
s=1
and
\gamma=-3
, analogues can be found in
[
40
]
).

Proposition 3.1
.

Let
0<s<1
,
\gamma>-3
and
\kappa>0
. Then

(3.7)

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}[(I-\boldsymbol{P})g]\lesssim\left\langle\mathcal{L}g,g\right\rangle_{L^{2}_{v}}\leq 2\left\langle\mathcal{L}_{1}g,g\right\rangle_{L^{2}_{v}}\lesssim\mathcal{A}[g],

(3.8)

\displaystyle\left\lVert g\right\rVert^{2}_{H^{s}_{v,\gamma/2}}+\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{L^{2}_{v,s+\gamma/2}}^{2}\lesssim\mathcal{A}[g]\lesssim\left\lVert g\right\rVert^{2}_{H^{s}_{v,s+\gamma/2}},

(3.9)

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}^{\kappa}[g]\lesssim\mathcal{A}[g].

Moreover, there exists a constant
0<\delta<1
such that

(3.10)

\left\langle\mathcal{L}_{2}g,h\right\rangle\lesssim\lVert\mu^{\delta}g\rVert_{L^{2}_{v}}\lVert\mu^{\delta}h\rVert_{L^{2}_{v}}.

In fact, an important bound we need, which is one of the main ingredients to prove (
3.7
)-(
3.8
), was given in
[
5
, Proposition 2.16]
.

Proposition 3.2
.

Let
\gamma>-3
. There exists a constant
C>0
such that

(3.11)

\frac{1}{10}\mathcal{A}[g]-C\left\lVert g\right\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}_{v,\gamma/2}}\leq\left\langle\mathcal{L}_{1}g,g\right\rangle_{L^{2}_{v}}\leq\mathcal{A}[g].

3.1.1.
Commutation properties

Since we are going to use an energy method, commutator estimates of the linearized operator with derivatives and weights are crucial.
For standard derivatives, these were already understood in e.g.
[
43
,
37
,
4
,
39
]
.
However, we need to be sure of analogous properties involving the vector field
Z
. Introducing the trilinear operator

(3.12)

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}(g,h,q)=\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}B(v-v_{*},\sigma)q_{*}\left(g^{\prime}_{*}h^{\prime}-g_{*}h\right){\rm d}v_{*}{\rm d}\sigma,

it is straightforward to check that

(3.13)

Z(\mathcal{T}(g,h,q))=\mathcal{T}(Zg,h,q)+\mathcal{T}(g,Zh,q)+\mathcal{T}(g,h,Zq).

Since
\Gamma(g,h)=\mathcal{T}(g,h,\sqrt{\mu})
, recalling the multi-index notation (
2.29
), we have the Leibniz formula

(3.14)

Z^{\beta}(\Gamma(g,h))=\sum_{|\beta_{1}|+|\beta_{2}|+|\beta_{3}|=|\beta|}C_{\beta_{1},\beta_{2},\beta_{3}}\mathcal{T}\left(Z^{\beta_{1}}g,Z^{\beta_{2}}h,(\nabla_{v})^{\beta_{3}}\sqrt{\mu}\right),

where we also used that
\mu
does not depend on
x
. Then we have the following adaptation of estimates obtained in
[
37
,
4
]
for the standard derivatives.

Lemma 3.3
.

There exist constants
C_{1},C_{2}>0
such that for any
\widetilde{\delta}>0
the following holds true:

(3.15)

\displaystyle|\langle{[\mathcal{L}_{1},Z^{\beta}]g,h}\rangle_{L^{2}_{v}}|

\displaystyle\leq\widetilde{\delta}\mathcal{A}[h]+\frac{C_{1}}{\widetilde{\delta}}\sum_{|\beta_{1}|\leq|\beta|-1}\mathcal{A}[Z^{\beta_{1}}g]

(3.16)

\displaystyle|\langle{[\mathcal{L}_{2},Z^{\beta}]g,h}\rangle_{L^{2}_{v}}|

\displaystyle\leq\widetilde{\delta}\lVert\mu^{\frac{1}{C(\beta)}}h\rVert_{L^{2}_{v}}^{2}+\frac{C_{2}}{\widetilde{\delta}}\sum_{|\beta_{1}|\leq|\beta|-1}\lVert\mu^{\frac{1}{C(\beta)}}Z^{\beta}g\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}_{v}},

where
C(\beta)=10^{4}(|\beta|+1)
. Moreover, let
\ell\geq 0
. There exists
0<\delta_{c}<1,\,C_{3},C_{4}>0
such that

(3.17)

\displaystyle|\langle{[\mathcal{L}_{1},\left\langle v\right\rangle^{\ell}]g,\left\langle v\right\rangle^{\ell}g}\rangle_{L^{2}_{v}}|

\displaystyle\lesssim\lVert\mu^{\delta_{c}}g\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}

(3.18)

\displaystyle\left\langle\left\langle v\right\rangle^{\ell}Z^{\beta}(\mathcal{L}g),\left\langle v\right\rangle^{\ell}Z^{\beta}g\right\rangle

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{100}\mathcal{A}[\left\langle v\right\rangle^{\ell}Z^{\beta}g]-C_{3}\sum_{|\beta_{1}|\leq\beta-1}\mathcal{A}[\left\langle v\right\rangle^{\ell}Z^{\beta_{1}}g]-C_{4}\lVert\mu^{\delta_{c}}Z^{\beta}g\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}_{v}}.

The same bound holds with
Z
replaced by
\nabla_{v}
.

Proof.

We first prove the bounds (
3.15
) and (
3.17
) involving
\mathcal{L}_{1}
. Recall that

\mathcal{L}_{1}g=-\Gamma(\sqrt{\mu},g)=-\mathcal{T}(\sqrt{\mu},g,\sqrt{\mu}).

Combining the Leibniz formula (
3.14
) with the fact that
\mu
does not depend on
x
, we get

(3.19)

[\mathcal{L}_{1},Z^{\beta}g]=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|\beta_{1}|+|\beta_{2}|+|\beta_{3}|=\beta\\
|\beta_{2}|\leq|\beta|-1\end{subarray}}C_{\beta_{1},\beta_{2},\beta_{3}}\mathcal{T}\left((\nabla_{v})^{\beta_{1}}\sqrt{\mu},Z^{\beta_{2}}g,(\nabla_{v})^{\beta_{3}}\sqrt{\mu}\right).

We thus have to estimate terms like

(3.20)

I=\left\langle\mathcal{T}\left((\nabla_{v})^{\beta_{1}}\sqrt{\mu},Z^{\beta_{2}}g,(\nabla_{v})^{\beta_{3}}\sqrt{\mu}\right),h\right\rangle.

From this point on, having
Z
-derivatives is the same as having
(x,v)
-derivatives in the proofs of the commutator estimates in
[
37
,
4
]
. For convenience of the reader, we present the main ideas to obtain such bounds. Notice that
{\partial}_{v_{i}}^{\beta_{j}}\sqrt{\mu}=P_{\beta_{j}}(v_{i})\sqrt{\mu}
for a polynomial
P_{\beta_{j}}
. Hence, with a slight abuse in notation we rewrite the term above as

(3.21)

I=\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}B(P_{\beta_{3}}\sqrt{\mu})_{*}((P_{\beta_{1}}\sqrt{\mu})_{*}^{\prime}Z^{\beta_{2}}g^{\prime}-(P_{\beta_{1}}\sqrt{\mu})_{*}Z^{\beta_{2}}g)h\,{\rm d}v{\rm d}v_{*}{\rm d}\sigma.

We split this term as
I=I^{1}+I^{2}
, where

(3.22)

\displaystyle I^{1}

\displaystyle=\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}B(P_{\beta_{3}}\sqrt{\mu})_{*}(P_{\beta_{1}}\sqrt{\mu})_{*}^{\prime}(Z^{\beta_{2}}g^{\prime}-Z^{\beta_{2}}g)h\,{\rm d}v{\rm d}v_{*}{\rm d}\sigma,

(3.23)

\displaystyle I^{2}

\displaystyle=\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}B(P_{\beta_{3}}\sqrt{\mu})_{*}((P_{\beta_{1}}\sqrt{\mu})_{*}^{\prime}-(P_{\beta_{1}}\sqrt{\mu})_{*})(Z^{\beta_{2}}g)h\,{\rm d}v{\rm d}v_{*}{\rm d}\sigma.

For
I^{2}
, combining Lemma
A.2
with Lemma
A.3
and using Cauchy-Schwarz we get

(3.24)

|I^{2}|\lesssim\lVert Z^{\beta_{2}}g\rVert_{L^{2}_{v,s+\gamma/2}}\left\lVert h\right\rVert_{L^{2}_{v,s+\gamma/2}}.

Concerning
I^{1}
, we consider the symmetric and antysimmetric part of the coefficients involving the Maxwellian, namely
I^{1}=I^{1}_{\mathrm{sym}}+I^{1}_{\mathrm{anti}}
with

(3.25)

\displaystyle I^{1}_{\mathrm{sym}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}BS(Z^{\beta_{2}}g^{\prime}-Z^{\beta_{2}}g)h\,{\rm d}v{\rm d}v_{*}{\rm d}\sigma,

(3.26)

\displaystyle S:

\displaystyle=(P_{\beta_{3}}\sqrt{\mu})_{*}(P_{\beta_{1}}\sqrt{\mu})_{*}^{\prime}+(P_{\beta_{3}}\sqrt{\mu})_{*}^{\prime}(P_{\beta_{1}}\sqrt{\mu})_{*},

(3.27)

\displaystyle I^{1}_{\mathrm{anti}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}BA(Z^{\beta_{2}}g^{\prime}-Z^{\beta_{2}}g)h\,{\rm d}v{\rm d}v_{*}{\rm d}\sigma,

(3.28)

\displaystyle A:

\displaystyle=(P_{\beta_{3}}\sqrt{\mu})_{*}(P_{\beta_{1}}\sqrt{\mu})_{*}^{\prime}-(P_{\beta_{3}}\sqrt{\mu})_{*}^{\prime}(P_{\beta_{1}}\sqrt{\mu})_{*}.

For the symmetric part, since
S
and
B
are invariant under the change
(v,v_{*})\to(v^{\prime},v_{*}^{\prime})
, we have

(3.29)

I^{1}_{\mathrm{sym}}=\frac{1}{4}\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}BS(Z^{\beta_{2}}g^{\prime}-Z^{\beta_{2}}g)(h-h^{\prime})\,{\rm d}v{\rm d}v_{*}{\rm d}\sigma.

To bound
S
, notice that for some
0<\kappa<1
(depending on
\beta_{3},\beta_{1}
) we have

(3.30)

|S|\lesssim\left(\mu_{*}\mu_{*}^{\prime}\right)^{\kappa/2}=\left(\mu\mu^{\prime}\right)^{\kappa/2}=\mu^{\kappa}+\mu^{\kappa/2}\left(\left(\mu^{\prime}\right)^{\kappa/2}-\mu^{\kappa/2}\right).

Hence, by the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality and the definition of
\mathcal{A}^{\kappa}
in (
3.6
), we infer

(3.31)

|I^{1}_{\mathrm{sym}}|\lesssim\mathcal{A}^{\kappa}[Z^{\beta_{2}}g]\mathcal{A}^{\kappa}[h]\lesssim\mathcal{A}[Z^{\beta_{2}}g]\mathcal{A}[h],

where we used (
3.9
) in the last inequality. To handle
I^{1}_{\mathrm{anti}}
, one exploits the fact that under the assumptions in the kernel, the change
v\to v^{\prime}
does not change the bounds. Thus, introducing further commutators, one is in a situation analogous to
I_{2}
. With the bounds above, appealing to Lemma
3.1
and using Young’s inequality, the commutator estimate (
3.15
) is proved.

Turning our attention to (
3.17
), notice that

(3.32)

\displaystyle\left\langle[\mathcal{L}_{1},\left\langle v\right\rangle^{\ell}]g,\left\langle v\right\rangle^{\ell}g\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}B\sqrt{\mu_{*}\mu_{*}^{\prime}}g^{\prime}g(\left\langle v^{\prime}\right\rangle^{\ell}-\left\langle v\right\rangle^{\ell})\left\langle v\right\rangle^{\ell}\,{\rm d}v{\rm d}v_{*}{\rm d}\sigma

(3.33)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}B(\mu_{*}\mu_{*}^{\prime})^{\frac{1}{4}}(\mu^{\frac{1}{4}}g)^{\prime}(\mu^{\frac{1}{4}}g)(\left\langle v^{\prime}\right\rangle^{\ell}-\left\langle v\right\rangle^{\ell})^{2}\,{\rm d}v{\rm d}v_{*}{\rm d}\sigma,

where in the last identity we used
\mu_{*}\mu_{*}^{\prime}=\mu\mu^{\prime}
and we symmetrized using
v\to v^{\prime}
. Since
2(\mu^{\frac{1}{4}}g)^{\prime}(\mu^{\frac{1}{4}}g)\leq((\mu^{\frac{1}{4}}g)^{\prime})^{2}+(\mu^{\frac{1}{4}}g)^{2}
, exchanging
v\to v^{\prime}
in the resulting integral of the first term, we get

(3.34)

\left|\left\langle[\mathcal{L}_{1},\left\langle v\right\rangle^{\ell}]g,\left\langle v\right\rangle^{\ell}g\right\rangle\right|\lesssim\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}B\left(\mu_{*}\mu_{*}^{\prime}\right)^{\frac{1}{4}}\left(\mu^{\frac{1}{4}}g\right)^{2}\left(\left\langle v^{\prime}\right\rangle^{\ell}-\left\langle v\right\rangle^{\ell}\right)^{2}\,{\rm d}v{\rm d}v_{*}{\rm d}\sigma.

By the mean value theorem

(3.35)

|\left\langle v^{\prime}\right\rangle^{\ell}-\left\langle v\right\rangle^{\ell}|\lesssim|v-v^{\prime}|(\left\langle v^{\prime}\right\rangle^{\ell-1}+\left\langle v\right\rangle^{\ell-1}).

Arguing as in (
A.15
), one has

(3.36)

|v-v^{\prime}|\lesssim\sin(\theta/2)|v-v_{*}|.

Therefore, combining (
3.35
) and (
3.36
) with Lemma
A.2
and Lemma
A.3
, from (
3.34
) we infer

(3.37)

\displaystyle|\left\langle[\mathcal{L}_{1},\left\langle v\right\rangle^{\ell}]g,\left\langle v\right\rangle^{\ell}g\right\rangle|\lesssim\lVert\mu^{\delta_{c}}g\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2},

where
\delta_{c}>0
is sufficiently small.

To prove (
3.16
), recalling that
\mathcal{L}_{2}g=-\Gamma(g,\sqrt{\mu})=-\mathcal{T}(g,\sqrt{\mu},\sqrt{\mu})
, we get

(3.38)

[\mathcal{L}_{2},Z^{\beta}f]=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|\beta_{1}|+|\beta_{2}|+|\beta_{3}|=|\beta|\\
|\beta_{2}|\leq|\beta|-1\end{subarray}}\mathcal{T}\left(Z^{\beta_{1}}f,(\nabla_{v})^{\beta_{2}}\sqrt{\mu},(\nabla_{v})^{\beta_{3}}\sqrt{\mu}\right).

Consequently, we can proceed as in the proof of
[
4
, Proposition 4.5]
(and
[
4
, Lemma 2.15]
) to prove (
3.16
). More precisely, one has to bound terms like

(3.39)

II=\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}B(P_{\beta_{3}}\sqrt{\mu})_{*}\left(Z^{\beta_{1}}g^{\prime}_{*}(P_{\beta_{2}}\sqrt{\mu})^{\prime}-Z^{\beta_{1}}g_{*}(P_{\beta_{2}}\sqrt{\mu})\right)h\,{\rm d}v{\rm d}v_{*}{\rm d}\sigma,

where
P_{\beta}
are again suitable polynomials arising from the derivatives of the Maxwellian.
Split
II=II^{1}+II^{2}
as

(3.40)

\displaystyle II^{1}

\displaystyle=\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}B(Z^{\beta_{1}}g)^{\prime}_{*}((P_{\beta_{3}}\sqrt{\mu})_{*}(P_{\beta_{2}}\sqrt{\mu})^{\prime}-(P_{\beta_{3}}\sqrt{\mu})_{*}^{\prime}(P_{\beta_{2}}\sqrt{\mu}))h\,{\rm d}v{\rm d}v_{*}{\rm d}\sigma,

(3.41)

\displaystyle II^{2}

\displaystyle=\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}B((P_{\beta_{3}}\sqrt{\mu}Z^{\beta_{1}}g)^{\prime}_{*}-(P_{\beta_{3}}\sqrt{\mu}Z^{\beta_{1}}g)_{*})(P_{\beta_{2}}\sqrt{\mu}h)\,{\rm d}v{\rm d}v_{*}{\rm d}\sigma.

For the term
II^{2}
one exploits the cancellation in
[
3
]
*Lemma 1 which says that

(3.42)

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}B(g_{*}^{\prime}-g_{*}){\rm d}v_{*}{\rm d}\sigma=(K*g)(v),\qquad K\approx|v|^{\gamma}.

For the term
II^{1}
it is enough to split the term containing the Maxwellians to apply the mean value theorem together with Lemmas
A.2
and
A.3
.

Finally, to prove (
3.18
), notice that

(3.43)

\displaystyle\left\langle v\right\rangle^{\ell}Z^{\beta}\mathcal{L}g=\mathcal{L}_{1}(\left\langle v\right\rangle^{\ell}Z^{\beta}g)+\mathcal{L}_{2}(\left\langle v\right\rangle^{\ell}Z^{\beta}g)+[\left\langle v\right\rangle^{\ell},\mathcal{L}_{1}+\mathcal{L}_{2}]Z^{\beta}g+\left\langle v\right\rangle^{\ell}[Z^{\beta},\mathcal{L}_{1}+\mathcal{L}_{2}]g.

Thanks to Proposition
3.2
and (
3.15
)-(
3.17
), in the formula above the only term we do not know how to estimate are the last ones containing
\mathcal{L}_{1}
on the right-side. However, multiplying (
3.19
) by
\left\langle v\right\rangle^{\ell}
corresponds in redefining
S
and
A
in (
3.26
) and (
3.28
) respectively as

(3.44)

\displaystyle S_{\ell}

\displaystyle:=(P_{\beta_{3}}\sqrt{\mu})_{*}(P_{\beta_{1}}\sqrt{\mu})_{*}^{\prime}\left\langle v\right\rangle^{\ell}+(P_{\beta_{3}}\sqrt{\mu})_{*}^{\prime}(P_{\beta_{1}}\sqrt{\mu})_{*}\left\langle v^{\prime}\right\rangle^{\ell}

(3.45)

\displaystyle A_{\ell}

\displaystyle:=(P_{\beta_{3}}\sqrt{\mu})_{*}(P_{\beta_{1}}\sqrt{\mu})_{*}^{\prime}\left\langle v\right\rangle^{\ell}-(P_{\beta_{3}}\sqrt{\mu})_{*}^{\prime}(P_{\beta_{1}}\sqrt{\mu})_{*}\left\langle v^{\prime}\right\rangle^{\ell}.

Moreover, also the term
I^{2}
in (
3.23
) has an extra
\left\langle v\right\rangle^{\ell}
multiplying the Maxwellian
\sqrt{\mu_{*}}
. Upon introducing further commutators if necessary, for instance in
A_{\ell}
, one can repeat the arguments done to prove (
3.15
) and obtain the same estimates (clearly with worst constants depending on
\ell
).

The fact that the lemma is true if we change
Z
with
\nabla_{v}
is straightforward. Indeed, as observed before, there is no difference between having
Z
or
\nabla_{v}
derivatives (the latter being used for the bounds in
[
37
,
4
]
).
∎

3.2.
Nonlinear operator

For the nonlinear part, the main trilinear estimate we are going to exploit was derived by Alexandre et al.
[
4
]
and Gressman and Strain
[
37
,
38
]
(obtained independently and with different techniques, see the discussion in
[
38
]
).

Theorem 3.4

(
[
38
]
*Theorem 2.1,
[
4
]
*Theorem 1.2)
.

Let
0<s<1
and
\gamma>\max\{-d,-d/2-2s\}
. Then

(3.46)

|\left\langle\Gamma(f,g),h\right\rangle|\lesssim\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{L^{2}_{v}}\sqrt{\mathcal{A}[g]\mathcal{A}[h]}.

For the commutation properties with the weight we have the following.

Proposition 3.5

(Proposition 3.13
[
4
]
)
.

Let
0<s<1
and
\gamma>\max\{-d,-d/2-2s\}
. Then, for any
\ell\geq 0
one has

(3.47)

\displaystyle\left|\left\langle\left\langle v\right\rangle^{\ell}\Gamma(f,g)-\Gamma(f,\left\langle v\right\rangle^{\ell}g),h\right\rangle\right|\lesssim

\displaystyle\sqrt{\mathcal{A}[h]}\big(\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{L^{2}_{v,s+\gamma/2}}\lVert\left\langle v\right\rangle^{\ell-s}g\rVert_{L^{2}_{v,s+\gamma/2}}

\displaystyle+\min\{\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{L^{2}_{v}}\lVert\left\langle v\right\rangle^{\ell-s}g\rVert_{L^{2}_{v,s+\gamma/2}},\,\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{L^{2}_{v,s+\gamma/2}}\lVert\left\langle v\right\rangle^{\ell-s}g\rVert_{L^{2}_{v}}\}\big)

Combining Theorem
3.4
, Proposition
3.5
and Lemma
3.3
, we get the following.

Lemma 3.6
.

Let
0<s<1
and
\gamma>\max\{-d,-d/2-2s\}
. Then, for any
B\geq 2d
,
M>B+|\gamma|+2s
,
|\beta|\leq B
one has

(3.48)

\displaystyle\left|\left\langle\left\langle v\right\rangle^{M}Z^{\beta}\Gamma(f,g),h\right\rangle\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim\sqrt{\mathcal{A}[h]}\sum_{|\beta_{1}|+|\beta_{2}|\leq|\beta|}\bigg(\lVert\left\langle v\right\rangle^{M}Z^{\beta_{1}}f\rVert_{L^{2}_{v}}\sqrt{\mathcal{A}[\left\langle v\right\rangle^{M}Z^{\beta_{2}}g]}

\displaystyle\hskip 113.81102pt+\sqrt{\mathcal{A}[\left\langle v\right\rangle^{M}Z^{\beta_{1}}f]}\lVert\left\langle v\right\rangle^{M}Z^{\beta_{2}}g\rVert_{L^{2}_{v}}\bigg)

Proof.

From (
3.14
), we see that we have to control terms like

(3.49)

\left\langle\left\langle v\right\rangle^{M}\mathcal{T}\left(Z^{\beta_{1}}f,Z^{\beta_{2}}g,(\nabla_{v})^{\beta_{3}}\mu\right),h\right\rangle.

We split this term as

(3.50)

\displaystyle\left\langle\left\langle v\right\rangle^{M}\mathcal{T}\left(Z^{\beta_{1}}f,Z^{\beta_{2}}g,(\nabla_{v})^{\beta_{3}}\mu\right),h\right\rangle

(3.51)

\displaystyle=\left\langle\left\langle v\right\rangle^{M}\mathcal{T}\left(Z^{\beta_{1}}f,Z^{\beta_{2}}g,(\nabla_{v})^{\beta_{3}}\mu\right)-\mathcal{T}\left(Z^{\beta_{1}}f,\left\langle v\right\rangle^{M}Z^{\beta_{2}}g,(\nabla_{v})^{\beta_{3}}\mu\right),h\right\rangle

(3.52)

\displaystyle\quad+\left\langle\mathcal{T}\left(Z^{\beta_{1}}f,\left\langle v\right\rangle^{M}Z^{\beta_{2}}g,(\nabla_{v})^{\beta_{3}}\mu\right),h\right\rangle.

Then, we notice that Theorem
3.4
and Proposition
3.5
are stated for
\Gamma(f,g)=\mathcal{T}(f,g,\sqrt{\mu})
. However, it is not difficult show that the bounds (
3.4
)-(
3.47
) holds true also for
\mathcal{T}(f,g,{\partial}_{v_{i}}^{\beta_{j}}\mu)
. Indeed, the proofs in
[
4
]
rely on a decomposition of the operator
\Gamma
based on a nice identity to isolate the singularities, see in
[
4
, Lemma 3.6]
. Upon properly symmetrizing to take care of the polynomial (as done for instance in (
3.26
)), using (
3.8
) and Proposition
3.1
, one can verify that the estimates (
3.46
)-(
3.47
) holds also for
\mathcal{T}(f,g,{\partial}_{v_{i}}^{\beta_{j}}\mu)
. Thus, we can apply the analogoue of Proposition
3.5
to the terms in (
3.51
) and Theorem
3.4
to the remaining ones.
∎

4.
Linear Estimates

This section is devoted to proving the results announced in Section
2.1
. A first crucial step is to obtain the monotonicity estimates (
2.6
) and (
2.15
) which we collect in the next proposition.

Proposition 4.1
.

Let
E^{e.d.}_{M,B}
,
\mathcal{D}^{e.d.}_{M,B}
,
E^{T.d.}_{M,B}
and
\mathcal{D}^{T.d.}_{M,B}
be the functionals defined in (
2.1
), (
2.5
), (
2.10
) and (
2.16
) respectively. Then, there exists constants
0<\delta_{e},\delta_{d}<1

(4.1)

\displaystyle\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}t}E^{e.d.}_{M,B}+\delta_{e}\mathcal{D}^{e.d.}_{M,B}\leq 0

(4.2)

\displaystyle\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}t}E^{T.d.}_{M,B}+\delta_{d}\mathcal{D}^{T.d}_{M,B}\leq 0.

We prove (
4.1
) and (
4.2
) in Section
4.1
and Section
4.2
respectively. Having these estimates at hand, in Section
4.3
we present the proof of the decay estimates for the linearized problem given in Theorem
2.4
.

To simplify the notation, since all the norms are
L^{2}_{v}
based, in the rest of this section we will always omit the subscript
L^{2}_{v}
and we write
H^{s}_{*}
instead of
H^{s}_{v,*}
. Moreover, thanks to (
2.1
) we know that the problem decouples in
k
.
Hence, the factors
\left\langle k\right\rangle^{\alpha}
in the definition of the energies play no role in the estimates (but are crucial in the nonlinear problem).

4.1.
Monotonicity estimate in the enhanced dissipation regime

The aim of this section is to prove (
4.1
) in Proposition
4.1
. As a consequence of the properties given in Section
3
, we first give some basic energy inequalities which are necessary to control the time-derivative of
E^{e.d.}_{M,B}
(
2.1
).

Lemma 4.2
.

For any
M>2|q_{\gamma,s}|
,
\beta\geq 0
, there hold the energy inequalities

(4.3)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}t}\left\lVert\left\langle v\right\rangle^{M}Z^{\beta}\hat{f}\right\rVert^{2}+\frac{\nu}{100}\mathcal{A}[\left\langle v\right\rangle^{M}Z^{\beta}\hat{f}]\leq\nu\mathcal{R}^{1}_{\beta},

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}t}\left\lVert\left\langle v\right\rangle^{M+q_{\gamma,s}}\nabla_{v}Z^{\beta}\hat{f}\right\rVert^{2}+\frac{\nu}{100}\mathcal{A}[\left\langle v\right\rangle^{M+q_{\gamma,s}}\nabla_{v}Z^{\beta}\hat{f}]

(4.4)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\leq\left|\left\langle\left\langle v\right\rangle^{M+q_{\gamma,s}}Z^{\beta}(ik\hat{f}),\left\langle v\right\rangle^{M+q_{\gamma,s}}\nabla_{v}Z^{\beta}\hat{f}\right\rangle\right|+\nu\mathcal{R}^{2}_{\beta},

(4.5)

\displaystyle\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}t}{\rm Re}\left\langle\left\langle v\right\rangle^{M+q_{\gamma,s}}Z^{\beta}(ik\hat{f}),\left\langle v\right\rangle^{M+q_{\gamma,s}}\nabla_{v}Z^{\beta}\hat{f}\right\rangle+|k|^{2}\left\lVert\left\langle v\right\rangle^{M+q_{\gamma,s}}Z^{\beta}\hat{f}\right\rVert^{2}\leq\nu\mathcal{R}^{3}_{\beta}.

where the remainder terms are given by

(4.6)

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}^{1}_{\beta}=C_{\beta,1}\bigg(\lVert\mu^{\delta}Z^{\beta}\hat{f}\rVert^{2}+\sum_{|\beta_{1}|\leq|\beta|-1}\mathcal{A}[\left\langle v\right\rangle^{M}Z^{\beta_{1}}\hat{f}]\bigg),

(4.7)

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}^{2}_{\beta}=C_{\beta,2}\bigg(\lVert\mu^{\delta}\nabla_{v}Z^{\beta}\hat{f}\rVert^{2}+\mathcal{A}[\left\langle v\right\rangle^{M+q_{\gamma,s}}Z^{\beta}\hat{f}]+\sum_{|\beta_{1}|\leq|\beta|-1}\mathcal{A}[\left\langle v\right\rangle^{M+q_{\gamma,s}}\nabla_{v}Z^{\beta_{1}}\hat{f}]\bigg),

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}^{3}_{\beta}=2\left|\left\langle\left\langle v\right\rangle^{M+q_{\gamma,s}}\nabla_{v}Z^{\beta}\hat{f},\left\langle v\right\rangle^{M+q_{\gamma,s}}Z^{\beta}\mathcal{L}(ik\hat{f})\right\rangle\right|

(4.8)

\displaystyle\qquad\quad+2\left|\left\langle[\nabla_{v},\left\langle v\right\rangle^{M+q_{\gamma,s}}]Z^{\beta}\hat{f},\left\langle v\right\rangle^{M+q_{\gamma,s}}Z^{\beta}\mathcal{L}(ik\hat{f})\right\rangle\right|,

with
0<C_{\beta,1},C_{\beta,2}
being fixed constants depending only on
\beta
.

Proof.

Bounds (
4.3
) and (
4.4
) are a consequence of (
3.18
). For (
4.5
), since

(4.9)

\displaystyle{\partial}_{t}\nabla_{x}Z^{\beta}f=-v\cdot\nabla_{x}\nabla_{x}Z^{\beta}f-\nu\nabla_{x}Z^{\beta}\mathcal{L}f,

(4.10)

\displaystyle{\partial}_{t}\nabla_{v}Z^{\beta}f+\nabla_{x}Z^{\beta}f=-v\cdot\nabla_{x}\nabla_{v}Z^{\beta}f-\nu\nabla_{v}Z^{\beta}\mathcal{L}f,

using the antisymmetry of
v\cdot\nabla_{x}
we get

(4.11)

\displaystyle\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}t}

\displaystyle{\rm Re}\left\langle\left\langle v\right\rangle^{M+q_{\gamma,s}}Z^{\beta}(ik\hat{f}),\left\langle v\right\rangle^{M+q_{\gamma,s}}\nabla_{v}Z^{\beta}\hat{f}\right\rangle+|k|^{2}\lVert\left\langle v\right\rangle^{M+q_{\gamma,s}}Z^{\beta}\hat{f}\rVert^{2}

(4.12)

\displaystyle=-\nu{\rm Re}\left\langle\left\langle v\right\rangle^{M+q_{\gamma,s}}Z^{\beta}\mathcal{L}(ik\hat{f}),\left\langle v\right\rangle^{M+q_{\gamma,s}}\nabla_{v}Z^{\beta}\hat{f}\right\rangle

(4.13)

\displaystyle\quad-\nu{\rm Re}\left\langle\left\langle v\right\rangle^{M+q_{\gamma,s}}Z^{\beta}(ik\hat{f}),\left\langle v\right\rangle^{M+q_{\gamma,s}}\nabla_{v}(Z^{\beta}\mathcal{L}\hat{f})\right\rangle.

Integrating by parts in
v
the last term above, and moving the
ik
on the term containing
\mathcal{L}
, we prove (
4.5
).
∎

We are now ready to prove the first bound in Proposition
4.1
.

Proof of Proposition
4.1
.

From (
2.1
) and (
4.3
)-(
4.5
), we get that

\displaystyle\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}t}E^{e.d.}_{M,B}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{200}\sum_{\alpha+|\beta|\leq B}\frac{2^{-\mathtt{C}\beta}\left\langle k\right\rangle^{\alpha}}{\left\langle\nu t\right\rangle^{2\beta}}\bigg(\nu\mathcal{A}[\left\langle v\right\rangle^{M}Z^{\beta}\hat{f}]+\nu a_{\nu,k}\mathcal{A}[\left\langle v\right\rangle^{M+q_{\gamma,s}}\nabla_{v}Z^{\beta}\hat{f}]

(4.14)

\displaystyle\quad+b_{\nu,k}|k|^{2}\left\lVert\left\langle v\right\rangle^{M+q_{\gamma,s}}Z^{\beta}\hat{f}\right\rVert^{2}\bigg)

(4.15)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\alpha+|\beta|\leq B}\frac{2^{-\mathtt{C}\beta}\left\langle k\right\rangle^{\alpha}}{\left\langle\nu t\right\rangle^{2\beta}}a_{\nu,k}\left|\left\langle\left\langle v\right\rangle^{M+q_{\gamma,s}}Z^{\beta}(ik\hat{f}),\left\langle v\right\rangle^{M+q_{\gamma,s}}\nabla_{v}Z^{\beta}\hat{f}\right\rangle\right|

(4.16)

\displaystyle\quad+\sum_{\alpha+|\beta|\leq B}\frac{2^{-\mathtt{C}\beta}\left\langle k\right\rangle^{\alpha}}{\left\langle\nu t\right\rangle^{2\beta}}(\nu\mathcal{R}^{1}_{\beta}+\nu a_{\nu,k}\mathcal{R}^{2}_{\beta}+\nu b_{\nu,k}\mathcal{R}^{3}_{\beta}),

where we have neglected the term with a negative sign on the right-hand side coming from the time derivative of
\left\langle\nu t\right\rangle^{-2\beta}
. In the rest of the proof, we highlight all the necessary restrictions on the coefficient and in the end we show that it is possible to choose the coefficients in order to satisfy said constraints.
We first bound the error terms in (
4.16
), which can be directly controlled with the available anisotropic dissipation appearing on the left-hand side of the inequalty.
Then we control the remaining mixed inner product error term in (
4.15
).

\diamond

Bound on

\mathcal{R}^{1}_{\beta}
(
4.6
). The sum containing lower order derivatives is controlled with the available dissipation coming from the
\beta-1
terms. Namely, it is enough to impose that

(4.17)

2^{-\mathtt{C}\beta}C_{\beta,1}\ll\frac{2^{-\mathtt{C}(\beta-1)}}{200}\quad\Longrightarrow\quad 2^{-\mathtt{C}}\ll\frac{1}{C_{\beta,1}}.

For the other term in
\mathcal{R}^{1}_{\beta}
, since
M+q_{\gamma,s}\geq 0
we have

(4.18)

\displaystyle\lVert\mu^{\delta}Z^{\beta}\hat{f}\rVert^{2}\lesssim\frac{1}{|k|^{2}}\left\lVert\left\langle v\right\rangle^{M+q_{\gamma,s}}Z^{\beta}(ik\hat{f})\right\rVert^{2}.

Thus, to control
\mathcal{R}^{1}_{\beta}
, we need to impose the following restrictions on the coefficients

(4.19)

\frac{\nu C_{\beta,1}}{|k|^{2}}\ll b_{\nu,k}.

\diamond

Bound on

\mathcal{R}^{2}_{\beta}
(
4.7
). To control the first term in the definition of
\mathcal{R}^{2}_{\beta}
, as in
[
4
, (6.14)]
, we exploit the following interpolation inequality: for any
\ell\geq-\gamma
,
\delta>0
there exists
C_{\delta}
such that

(4.20)

\displaystyle\lVert\left\langle v\right\rangle^{\ell}\nabla_{v}g\rVert_{L^{2}_{\gamma/2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\delta\lVert\left\langle v\right\rangle^{\ell}\nabla_{v}g\rVert_{H^{s}_{\gamma/2}}^{2}+C_{\delta}\lVert\left\langle v\right\rangle^{\ell}\nabla_{v}g\rVert_{H^{-1}_{\gamma/2}}^{2},\lesssim\delta\mathcal{A}[\left\langle v\right\rangle^{\ell}\nabla_{v}g]+C_{\delta}\lVert\left\langle v\right\rangle^{\ell}g\rVert_{L^{2}_{\gamma/2}}^{2}.

The bounds above follow by the Gagliardo-Nirenberg inequality and straightforward commutator estimates to handle the weights.
With the inequality (
4.20
) at hand, we get

(4.21)

\displaystyle C_{\beta,2}\lVert\mu^{\delta}\nabla_{v}Z^{\beta}\hat{f}\rVert^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{400}\mathcal{A}[\left\langle v\right\rangle^{M+q_{\gamma,s}}\nabla_{v}Z^{\beta}\hat{f}]+\frac{\widetilde{C}_{\beta,2}}{|k|^{2}}\left\lVert\left\langle v\right\rangle^{M+q_{\gamma,s}}Z^{\beta}(ik\hat{f})\right\rVert^{2}.

Moreover, since
q_{\gamma,s}\leq 0
we have

(4.22)

\mathcal{A}[\left\langle v\right\rangle^{M+q_{\gamma,s}}Z^{\beta}f]\lesssim\mathcal{A}[\left\langle v\right\rangle^{M}Z^{\beta}f].

Hence, we require that

(4.23)

\frac{\nu a_{\nu,k}\widetilde{C}_{\beta,2}}{|k|^{2}}\ll b_{\nu,k},\qquad a_{\nu,k}C_{\beta,2}\ll 1,\qquad 2^{-\mathtt{C}}C_{\beta,2}\ll 1,

in order to be able to absorb the errors terms with the dissipation.

\diamond

Bound on

\mathcal{R}^{3}_{\beta}
(
4.8
). From the upper bound in (
3.7
), we get

|\left\langle\mathcal{L}g,h\right\rangle|\lesssim\sqrt{\mathcal{A}[g]}\sqrt{\mathcal{A}[f]}.

Hence, using
M>|q_{\gamma,s}|
and
-d<\gamma<0
, from the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality we deduce

(4.24)

\nu b_{\nu,k}\mathcal{R}^{3}_{\beta}\leq\frac{\nu}{1600}\mathcal{A}[\left\langle v\right\rangle^{M}Z^{\beta}\hat{f}]+\nu C|k|^{2}(b_{\nu,k})^{2}\big(\mathcal{A}[\left\langle v\right\rangle^{M+q_{\gamma,s}}\nabla_{v}Z^{\beta}\hat{f}]+\mathcal{A}[\left\langle v\right\rangle^{M}Z^{\beta}\hat{f}]\big)

Consequently, the following restriction on the coefficients is needed

(4.25)

C|k|^{2}(b_{\nu,k})^{2}\ll\min\{a_{\nu,k},1\}.

Collecting the estimates on the remainders made above, under the restrictions (
4.19
),(
4.23
) and (
4.25
), we can absorb all the
\mathcal{R}^{i}_{\beta}
error terms to get

\displaystyle\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}t}E^{e.d.}_{M,B}
